To Wirom IT BELONGED.-Sr Robert Ball has an amusing story of a meteorite which fell on an American farm. It was claimed by the ground landlord, because the lease reserved to him all minerais and metals on the land. It was also claimed by the tenant on the score that it was not in the ground when the lease was made. The landlord than required it as "flying game." The tenant, however, pleaded that it had neither wings not feathers, and asserted his right to it as ground game. While the dispute was raging, the revcrne officers seized it as an article uhich had been introduced into the country without payment of duty.

GRATEFUL-COM FORTI NG.

COCO

4REAKFAST AND SUPPER:

LADIES' LETTEIL

MY DEAR NELLIE, There is a decided tendency to put more fulness into skirts near the waist, and this fashion is very pretty and becoming if it is carried out in thia materials, such as soft silks, voiles, or nun's veil- logs. A very pretty gown is made of pale mauve China silk, trimmed with ficelle-coloured lace ; it is lined throughout with glace silk to match the lace, and has gathered frills of pinked. out silk at the edge of the underskirt. The foun- dation fits closely round the hips, and the over- skirt only is gathered finely several times, leaving a fiat space in front. The draperies of the bodice are also gathered, and they open over a vest of tucked white chiffon; there are also puffs of white chiffon at the wrists. The lower part of the skirt is cut separately from the upper part, and forms a fluted flounce, which has rows of tucks at the edge ; the join is hidden by the lace appliques which appear at the head of the flounce. A soft sash of chiffon is tied round the waist, and long ends hang down to the bottom of the skirt.

I have noticed that hardly any bodices are now made high to the throat, being cut away and filled in with white muslin or insertion. The idea is that the dress should come up over this, but too often the gown is sewn down upon it. This destroys the idea, but it is often so in dressmaking. As to boas, they scarcely dare call themselves so, unless they are provided at either extremity with many long strands of chenille, reaching almost to the hem of the wearer's gown. These chenille ends have their uses. They weight the boa or ruffle, and prevent it•silently slipping off the shoulders, aud absenting themselves without leave.

An extremely smart costume is made of Wedg- wood-blue eolionne, over an underdress of the palest rose-pink silk, in one of the soft drages shack-4, veiled with creamy guipure or Irish lace. The edges of the draperies are stitched two or three times, and the points are fastened with old silver buttons. The drawn waistbelt is of soft black satin, cut on the cross, and the strays in front are formed of black satin ribbons, with little bows at the top. This elegant model, which would be very becoming to a woman with a fine figure, should be lined through with glace or foundation silk of a drag& shade of pink. The design conic be carried out equally well in silk cashmere, a thin faced cloth, or other light woollen materials, and it is also suitable for various other combinations of colour. A material in pale turquoise or electric blue would look very pretty over the cream lace underdress with a lining of ivory-white, and a dress of white cloth or cashmere would be effective with an underdress of 6cru or coffee-eoloured lace, mounted on a white satin lining, with a waistbelt and ribbon:, in turquoise-blue.

It is generally agreed that hats were never prettier than they are this summer. I should like to describe three which particularly impressed me the other day. The first was of fine, flexible straw, with a trimming of pink ribbons and roses, the latter nestling up against the high crown. The lace fell slightly over the edge of the brim, which was lined underneath with pink silk muslin or chiffon. A black velvet bandeau raised the crown slightly from the hair. The second was of the beb6 order, and had a straw crown, with kilted chiffon frills for brim. All the frills were edged with lace. The high silk bow was wired. The third hat was in sunburned straw, edged with black velvet. The rosette was in crimson silk, and the curled quills were dark brown. A cluster of black- berries rested en the hair at the left side, under the brim.

The little black silk coats which are being worn are very smart, and will be much in evidence when the weather gets a little cooler. Some of them are trimmed with embroidery or colour. That worn by a very prominent personage the other day was ornamented with antique embroidery on blue cloth in a very effective way. Another is faced with white cloth, embroidered with large raised flowers outlined with black, filled in with white and veined with pale gold thread. Cloth may be considered rather heavy for this purpose, but it is not so, in fact, for that chosen is of so light a texture as to be quite as free from weight as the glactS silk of which the little garment i• composed.

Your loving,

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

That the happiest hours of her Majesty's long and glorious reign have been spent amid the simple privacy of family life, in the country retreats she has purchased for herself, there is no possible doubt. And can we marvel that this should be so? When all is said and done, the Queen is but flesh and blood, nerves and heart like the humblest and most irresponsible of her subjects, and it must, at times have been nothing but the short intervals of repose she has been able to snatch at Balmoral and Osborne, which has enabled her to bear, with such grand courage, and wonderful self restraint, the hear and burden of that long day, which happily is not yet finished. The Prince of Wales bad arranged to go from Osborne to Marienbad, but, learning that the Duc d' Orleans was there, His Royal Highness changed his plans,and decided t 3 go to Homburg for three weeks. Another considera- tion is that the Prince is anxious to be near the Empress Frederick, whew health unfortunately is just now a further cause for anxiety to our Queen and Royal Family. On his return tretn the Continent, the Prince of Wales will go to Scotland,but the Princess will remain abroad for some time, paying her usual annual visits to the various members of her family in Denmark. This year the grouse obtained one day's grace, as the Twelfth fell upon a Sunday, but, as a rule, it is astoniEh- ing how soon the birds reach London after the shootine is supposed to begin. Usually grouse eau be had for lunch on the opening day anywhere in London,which is fully twenty-four hours before the birds reach the Royal table at Osborne. There are not many grouse on the Balmoral and Abergeldie estates, and there are never any Royal shooting-parties for the Twelfth. Tho moors are shot over by the keepers, simply in order that a large box of grouse may be sent at once to Osborne. King Victor Emmanuel 111, and Queen Helena have surely won the hearts of the Italian people by their prompt and kindly sympathy and assistance at the scene of the terrible railway atcident near Rome: So soon as their Majesties heard of the accident they left the Palace, and joined in the work of rescuing the wounded, and sootning their sufferings. The Queen's sister, and her husband the grand Duke Peter of Russia, were in the wrecked train, and both happily escaped injury, but the accident was of a more than usually disastrous kind, the killed and wounded numbering con- siderably more than a hundred. . . _

Victor Emmanuel 111 is of a somewhat frail and delic- ate appearance, and he has all the reserve of a retiring disposition. Be is both small of stature, and slightly built, though at the age of 31, he would no doubt be considered to be in the prime of life. His marriage with the Princess Helena four years ago, was extremely popu- lar in Italy, but there is perhaps one thine in which Her Majesty hes disappointed her people. Whenever Royalties go abroad the populace always looks for them to wear a smile, and as Net the Italian people says they have never succeeded in making Queen Helena look pleased. This is of course, not from want of any syni- pathy er interest in the people, as recent events have proved beyond doubt. It is her habit to look somewhat preternaturally serious, and she has never quite suc- ceeded in overcoming this inclination when seen in public. The mosquito though prevalent in many places, especially in London, has net as yet proved so great a pest as it was last summer. There is consequently some hope that after all our climate may be too much for it. This would he all the more satisfactory as scientific in- vestigations all tend to show that it is the cause of much mischief. Not only is it said to be the cause of malaria but it is also accused of spreading other dis- eases, including elephantiasia, that hideous deformity se common in the East,and which causes the limbs to swell to double or treble their ordinary size. It is when we come to the remedy, that the crusade against the mos- quite seems so hopeless. The only thing suggesTed as yet, is its extermination but there are few countries in the world in which the mosquito does not flourish, and one is tempted to ask how such a thing would be possible. We might just as well try to exterminate summer flier, which would in fact be child's play, as compared with the annihilation of the mosquito. Many School Boards have recently made systematic efforts to discourage juvenile smoking. There is another thing they might do, which would be at least equally as useful as trying to exterminate the mosquito, and that is the discouragement of the habit among children, of putting pencils in their mouthsein order to make them I mark better. When the pencils are given out indis- criminately, the child constantly getting a different one, there is some danger of taking a complaint or disease, just as there is frcm the practice of licking the finger tl turn over the pages of a book or periodical. The best thing would be to make all pencils bitter or tasty to the taste. 90 that no one would put them twice in the mouth but that, like the extermination of the mosquitoes is perhaps hardly practicable. Rarely indeed do we see a woman cyclist three ding her way in crowded thoroughfares nowadays. Cycles are restricteJ to country use only—when they are in- valuable—and to ride ore in crowded towns and cities is now deemed an undignified and foolhardy proceeding. That more accidents did not occur when the craze for cycling at all hours, and in all places was at its heieht„is little short of marvellous. All the skill in the world can scarcely insure a cyclist against a side slip in wet weather,on a greasy road,ard if the accident takes place when hemmed in by vehicles, it is more than probable that tht cyclist will be very seriously injured. Why will so many women persist in leaving their coats behind when starting for a long spin ? During the uncertain weather which preceded this last heat wave, it was by no means an uncommon event to pass cyciists on a conetry read, miles from " anywhere " ia a muslin blouse, or a cotton shirt, drenched. In a climate such as we enjoy (?) in which we are scorched one day and almost drowned and blown away the next, we can Scarcely conceive of people So toalish as not to make provision for the weather when starting on a ride of any length. A skirt for cycling should invariably be built of Pirle-finished fabric, which will defy the heaviest of rain. Add to this a Viyella blouse or shirt, a neat little waterproof coat, and sailor hat, and we need have no fear of the elements. By the way.there is Special waterproof leather glove being sold for cyclists. Its price, however, is of somewhat exalted character at present. The general mourning ordered by Her Majesty has been widely observed, few colours being en Evidence during the peat three weeks. Mourning nowadays does not meen FO much the adoption of black, as the avoid- ance of colour. A white Roza I.,ith a few yards of black tete ribbon run through the insertions, a grey gown trimmed with white, accompanied by the all fashionable black hat, or perchance a heliotrope frock with a black RR‘il, and a touch of black at the neck, is all the mourning that is required of U 9, save fir a neat relative. Children are seldom, if ever seen in blank gar- Mentr, and this applies especially to Royal childrat. The trand-children of the late Duke of Saxe-Cohurg are, it has been noticed, wearing white, and neither the Czarina the Duchess of Fife, or the Duchess of York have ever petmitted their children to be dressed in blank. ENGLISH WOMEN "ALL NERVES."

A Note of Alarm.

There are ominous signs that the conditions of press-IV:- day life induce a state of extreme nervous tension in wornee, especially married women. The danger threatens not only the peace of many homes but the welfare ef the next generation also. One instance is that of a lady at East Elam, who has been the victim of dis tressing nerve troutles since the birth of her first child eight years ago. The following details were furnished by Mrs. Shaw, of 92, Lancaster-road, East Barn. Ezsex, to a reporter ol the "Essex Guardian.". She stated : " Bly nerves caused all the troub'e, and made my life almost unendursble. At times I was quite unable to control myself. As goon as the children had gone to schsol. the house became unbearable, and I had to go nut. I knew that I was neglecting the home, but could net help it. Doctors told me to' pull myself together,' and to wercome my feelings,' but it was impossible. I was a patient at several hospitals, but as time went on I got moue iestead of better. I had palpitation of the heart, and suffered from shortness of breath. I got very thin, had attAcka of faintness, and the nervousness increased so that I dare not be left alone aster dark. THE TEMPTATION OF DUNCAN RUTLAND.

ar JOSEPH HOCKING,

CHAPTER XIX.

WDAT SZLIZON AND DUNCAN SAW AT ST. I'ETER'A

"You teem surprised to see me," said the girl, with a laugh. . "I am, indeed," he replied. "I had no ides that you thought. of coming here." "No! Well, everything has been very sadder It is less than a week ago since mother decided to come. The truth is she has not been very well, and as winter does not come to an end in Lancashire till well on to the end of April, the doctor ordered her south. She has not been to Rome for many years, while I had never been at all, so she decided that we should come here. Of course, you can imagine how delighted I am to come and see this,. the great centre of our faith." _ _

"Oh, yes," replied Rutland, "and I am gladder than words can say to see you here. I hope her ladyship is better.' "Yes, she is better, but she is far from strong. The doctor advised Italy, while Father Ritzoom, who has been staying at the Priory, persuaded her to come to Rome. He says it is one of thr healthiest. cities in the world."

"I should judge that he is right," said Rutland. "Of course' before the Italian Government took possession Rome was a fever-trap, but now it it wonderfully clean and health-giving." "And are you better?" asked Alizon, kindly. "Oh, yes, I am better." "It seems such a long time since I saw you. Why, it was last September when we last met, nearly six months ago. "Yes, it is a long time. It seems six years." "Does it? And you have been very ill. I-1 was very anxious about you. We often made inquiries." "You are very kind," replied the young man. "We heard once that you were dead," said the girl; "and—and mother immediately sent a special messenger to your lodgings. I—l was so relieved to know that the report was false." "Thank you," replied Rutland. "I suppose at one time the doctor thought I had breathed my last. But there—it's not worth talking about now. lam worth a host of dead men. I'm not going to die yet." During dinner Dady Neville proved a delightful companion. She related some of her former experiences in Rome, how as a girl she had an audience with Pope Pius IX., what he had said to her, and how he had impressed her. "I was here on my honeymoon with Sir Charles," she said, gaily, "so naturally everything appeared to me in the brightest of colours. Besides, the Holy Father was very kind, as were some of the Cardinals. At that time there was a great movement towards Rome. As you know, Newman and Manning brought a troop of converts at their heels, and it appeared as though the whole country was coming to bow the knee to the Holy Father. It seemed to atone for the lozs of Imperial power in the State." "But Rome has changed since then," suggested Rut land.

"I suppose it has, doubtless it has. But tell me what you have been doing here. Father Ritzoc,o3 told me he had written to Father Rizzio asking him to render you any service in his power. I hope he has done so."

Thereupon Rutland told her what he had seen. How he had been led from the head of one organisation to another, and how he had been astounded at the vast magnitude of the machinery of the Church.

"And were these men interesting?" asked her ladyship, her eyes beaming with pleasure and satisfaction.

Thereupon Rutland spoke of the services which had been rendered him in no sparing terms. He extolled very hithly the learning and intellectual power of the men with whom he had been brought into contact.

"The Church occupies a different position here from what it does in England," said her ladyship. "Yes, in a sense the Church is almighty," replied Rutland. "The wealth of the world has been poured into this city. Everything astounds one." "I am so glad you came here," she replied. "Father Ritzoom feared it might give you a wrong impression, and--" She hesitated a second as though she had said more than she had intended. "You see," she went on, "Rome is not like other cities. It cannot be understood from the outside. It is like a church window. You cannot see its beauty from the outside; you must go inside and look out towards the light." "I think I understand, Lady Neville," he replied; but he did not understand at all. "I am afraid I am not well enough to take much exercise while lam hex-6," said her ladyship. "The doctor ordered we perfect rest, but I am anxious that Alizon shall see everything. You say that Father Matthew is accompanying you?" "Yes," he replied, "and—and," he stammered almost painfully, "if Miss Alizon can accompany us—l—l shall be delighted." "That will be capital," responded her ladyship. "To-morrow and Monday Father Matthew will be engaged," went on Rutland, "and I had decided to wander around by myself. To-morrow morning I thought of going to high Mass at Peter's." "0 mother, I should like to go," said tilizon, eagerly. "Well, why not?" responded her ladyship, "that is if Mr. Rutland will encumber himself with you." They sat talking during dinner, and then Lady Neville invited him to her private drawing-room, where they passed the evening most pleasantly. On the following morning the two started for St. Peter's. They had allowed themselves plenty of time, and so were able to walk quietly. The sky was clear and bright, the air was crisp and exhilarating. When they reached the Piazza Del Popolo the morning sunlight fairly flooded the square, while up above them in the gardens of the Fincio the evergreens glinted in the light of the bright sun. "This is lovely," cried the young man. "I never dreamt of such happiness yesterday." "1 am awfully excited," said the girl. "I have often dreamt of being in Rome on a Sunday, and longed to attend high Mass at St. Peter's. Haven't you? " "No, lcannot say that I have," replied Rutland. "Oh, I forgot. Ido pity you, Mr. Rutland." "I pity myself," responded the young man. "This is the Corso," he continued, as they walked down the long thoroughfare. "This afternoon, indeed on all afternoons, it is crowded with carriages. So is the Pincio above. Yonder is the house which Dumas had in his mind when he described Monte Cristo watching the carnival," and he went on pointing out objects of interest as they went down the Corso. As they walked across the square outside the Vatican buildings Alizon spoke never a word. She seemed entranced at the sight of the great building. The fivures of Christ and His Apostles standing on the great front of the church, looking solemnly across the city, filled her mind with many thoughts ; the ground on which she walked was to her holy. When they came to the massive bronze door& which were open only on great occasions, sha felt a shiver pass through her frame, then she followed Rutland to a smaller entrance, where a dirty Italian lifted a heavy pad which hung across the doorway, and,"then held out his cap for alms. "Starving beggars on the stops of St. Peter's, - she nuirmured, as she passed in. Such thoughts were dispelled, however, when she stood beneath the roof of the most renowned church in Christendom. She saw at a altine9 that untold wealth had been poured here. Staities pillars, decorations, all represented incalculablc riches.

________ Duncan made hi i way towards the Gregorian Chapel, where high Mass was being celebrated. it is only a small building, capable of holding, perhaps, two or three hundred people. Very little accommodatiori was allowed for worshippers, how- ever. A few forms were placed, calculated to seat forty or fifty .people, but beyond that, nothing. About a hundred and fifty people were gathered, nearly all of whom were standing. At each end of the chapel sat a number of priests chanting. There were eighty in all, forty at each end of the build- ing, who sat in the armed seats provided. But few worshippers appeared to be present, nearly all were spectators. Both Ali= and Duncan noted that all the priests belonged to the higher order of clergy. Bishops, Canons, Monsignori, and other digiitaries participated in the service. Ail of them wore magniacent robes.

-

Allzon looked around tor a place Ivnereon to kneel, but noticing that all remained standing and looked towards the clergy, she did likewise. Presently she found herself studying their races. She remembered that all of them belonged to the aristocratic portion of the clergy, some were even princes of the Church, and yet she failed to be awed. There was scarcely a fine face among them. Most of the priests were fat, corpulent men. As she watched she saw them yawning as they chanted the words from the books they held, as though they were weary, and longed for the cere- mony to come to an end. A cold, disappointing feeling came into her heart. She expected to be awed by this service; she had dreamt of kneeling down on the floor of St. Peter's, while a great hush filled the hearts of the multitude. Here, however, all was different. There was no reverent feeling pervading the place. Presently a feeling of indignation came into her heart; for while the officiating priest stood on the altar and was handling the sacred elements, she saw that the crowd of clergy who sat in the stalls were taking their snuff-boxes from beneath their garments and deliberately ,taking snuff in the presence of the amused spectators. Involuntarily she looked towards Rutland and noticed a smile playing around his lips, a smile partly of amusement, partly of derision. She continued to watch, however. She had Intended to fall on her knees at the elevation of the host, but even while the officiating priest was engaged in what she believed to be the most sacred part of his duty, the changing of the elements into the very blood and body of Christ, she saw these prelates of the Church whispering to each other, yawning, and deliberately taking snuff. At that moment she felt someone tugging at her skirts, and looking down she saw a poor Italian woman on her knees, and was trying to make her two children kneel likewise. It was in doing this that she had accidentally pulled Alizon's dress. The sight of the woman aroused in her a sense of duty, and so she knelt down, and tried to think the thoughts appropriate to the solemn occasion. But it was impossible. As she read the book before her she pictured those dignitaries of the Church, there within the very walls of St. Peter's, yawning, and indulging in a habit which she despised, even while they believed that the very body and blood of Christ were elevated in their midst. She did not condemn the habit so much in private life, but that they should indulge in it within those sacred walls on such an occasion, seemed to the English girl the veriest sacrilege. Presently the service came to an end, and she had to move nearer Rutland, while a dozen or more priests entered, followed by boys. Then a proces- sion was formed. The officiating priest carried a receptacle containing the sacred elements, over this was placed a canopy borne by four men, while others bore candles, and thus they paraded around the church. She had enjoyed these processions in England, they had seemed to her picturesque and stately, but here everything seemed mechanical, unreal. All was so different from what she expected. "What does Mr. Rutland think of it, I wonder ? " she asked herself, but she did not ask him, somehow a weight seemed on her tongue.

Presently they found themselves wandering round the church. Many hundreds of people were within its walls, but it appeared almost empty. "Is it not stupendous ? " said Rutland. "Yes, it is immense," she replied, quietly; ".but I think I like smaller churches best." "You see those windows there at the left ? " said Rutland, when they had gone outside, pointing to the Vatican buildings.

"Yes." "The third from the end belongs to the Pope's private apartment."

"Indeed."

"Report has it that he often stands there at night-time, and looks out over the city, grieving at its loss, and dreaming of a time when he will not only reign over Rome again, but over all the world."

"And do you think he ever will ? " "No, he never will," replied the young man, quietly. :She seemed about to reply, but somehow the words died on her lips. Why, she could not tell. "What do you think of the church and the service ? " asked Rutland, presently. "St. Peter's is terribly imposing," she replied, "only—" "Only what? " "I do not know why, but—but it feels like a gorgeous vault." "Do you feel like that, too?" asked Rutland. "Oh, do not mistake me," said the girl, impulsively. "It is all very grand. Such a church ought to be. When 1 have gone again and again I shall understand it better. But I have never been in Rome before, and I seem to be stunned."

"Yes, I can quite understand you." They walked quietly back to the hotel, scarcely speaking a word. They took a circuitous route, passing the Pantheon and the Piazza Venezia on their way, Alizon constantly looking around with an expression of bewilderment on her face. "It's wonderfully grand," she said presently.

"That is the word, replied Rutland: "It will take me a few days to get accustomed to it all. At present lam sure Ido not see things as they ought to be seen," and , she sighed as though she were disaupointed. Presently they came to the Piazza Di Spagna, which was glittering in the sunlight, and where flower-sellers had brought their beauteous wares. Bright-eyed Italian girls followed them, beseeching them to buy, using all the English words they knew.

"I like this part of Rome best," cried Alizon, "it seems more like home."

"But not like home on a Sunday," suggested Rutland.

For several days after this nothing of importance happened, save that Duncan was invited to meet two of the dignitaries of the Church who had promised to dine with Lady Neville. These gentle- men, Monsignor Lindly and the Bishop of Tolisco, impressed the young man greatly, but both he and Alizon forgot the many things ,these priests spoke about, owing to the fact that on their return from sight-seeing one day, they were met with the dread news that Lady Neville had been taken seriously ill.

CHAPTER XX.

THE BAIIBINO.

Rutland did not see Alizon for some hours after their return. No sooner did she hear of the dread news than she rushed to her mother's room, and nothing would induce her to leave the bedside. It appears that just after lunch Lady Neville had been seized with severe pains, while her blood suddenly rose to fever heat. Every endeavour had beer made to find Alizon, but in vain. No one knew what part of the city she was visiting,, and as Lady Neville had become delirious she could give nc information. The • nearest doctor had been sent for, who had shaken his head gravely, and sug- gested that he consulted immediately with an English doctor who resided in Rome. The two physicians were holding a consultation when Alizon arrived. They agreed concerning a prescrip- tion, and left about nine o'clock, saying they would call again in the morning, but that if her ladyship was taken worse through the night they were to be immediately sent for.

About midnight her ladyship appeared to become quieter, and dropped into a fitful sleep, and it was not until then that Alizon left the room. When she got into the corridor she saw Rutland walking up and down, and with a grateful heart she went towards him. As far as she knew he was the only friend she had in the city, and this fact drew her more closely to him. "I think mother is a little better," she cried.

"I am very glad," replied the young man.' "Have you seen the doctors?" "Yes, I saw them as they came out." "Did you speak to them ? " "Yes. I asked them a good many questions." "And what did they say ? " "Oh, I judged that they regarded the matter hopefully, but they seemed very reticent. They always are, you know." • "Are they? 1 know so little of doctors. But— but you think mother will get better, don't you?" "I hope so, I trust so," said Rutland. "I have been praying for you, Miss Alizon, and if I can render you a service you will not hesitate to ask me. will you? " "Oh, thank you. You—you are the only one I could ask in Rome. Mother has friends here, but I do not know them. But it is late. Ought you not to go to bed ? " "I could not sleep if I wont. I have stayed here in the corridor hoping that I might be able to help you." "Have you? I am very glad. I know it is selfish of me. I am sure you will," she added.

She is constantly telling me to send for a priest, too. Do you think I ought, Mr. Rutland?"

"At any rate, one would do no harm," replied the young man. "Her ladyship will very likely be conscious by morning, and the presence of a good man might comfort her." "Sister Mary says she knows such a holy priest, a Franciscan, and she tells me that his prayers have wrought wondrous cures."

"The irayers of a good man must do good," replied Duncan. "But the doctors will be here early in the morning; you can ask them." "Yes, I will; but I must go back now, and you ought to go to bed. You have only just recovered from an illness yourself, you know." "No, I shall not go to bed; but I will lie down on a sofa in your drawing-room if you will let me. I shall be near you then, and ready to render you any service if you need me." "Oh, you are kind!" said the girl. "Good- night." "Good-night, and remember that God is good. Nothing happens by chance. He never makes a mistake." "Are you sure?" ...._ __ . __

"Yes, 'I am sure. I shall be praying for you." The girl went back to her mother's room. In spite of her anxiety, she felt better for her talk with Rutland. True, he was not a Catholic; but she felt sure that his prayers would help her. He was calm and strong too, and this helped her. She was glad, too, that he was close by. His near- ness comforted her. She looked at her mother, and saw that she slept heavily, while the two nurses stood near. One, a nun, prayed audibly, and counted her beads.

At eight o'clock in the morning, the doctors came and held another consultation. Just as they were leaving, Rutland spoke to them. "How do you find her ladyship ? " he asked. "She is very ill," was the reply. "Worse ? " "No, we should think she is rather better." "You think she will recover ? "

"Oh, yes, I should say so," replied the English doctor. "In about thirty hours from now the lever will leave her, and then she will get better. But in all such cases the fever must run its course."

"I think her daughter would like her to see a priest. I suppose there is no objection to that ? " The Italian doctor smiled scornfully. Like most of the scientific men of Italy, he was an Atheist. "Let her, by all means," he said, flippantly; "it can do no harm. We shall cure her, and the priest will get the credit." "Pardon me, are you in any way related to Lady Neville?" asked the English doctor. "No; but I knew them in England, and they regard me as a friend." "Nothing more, 1 suppose? I do not ask because of mere curiosity." • "No, nothing more." "You have no influence with Miss Neville? " "No, none in particular." "I am sorry for that, because—but are you a Catholic?" "No, I am not a Catholic."

"Of course, the fever may leave her a few hours before I said, and when it does, anything like feat would do her incalcuable harm." "7 understand." "That is well." The doctors left the room together. _ The MAYOR, in seconding the adoption of the minutes, emphasized what Alderman Sutton had said with regard to their relations with the Manchester Port Sanitary Authority. From the very beginning they had net attempted in the slightest degree to shirk their share of I the responsibility or of the expense. The only question was 38 to what their share should be, and he did not think there could be any posiible difference of opinion that the question should be decided in proportion to the tonnage arriving at the various ports. To take the rate- able value of the ports as the basis would absurd be- cause as they knew W'arringtun was not a seagoing port except in a very limited sense indeed, and to assess the proportion of contribution upon the rateable value of the town alone when the town derived so little bee- fit from seagoing traffic would be a monstrous proposal —(hear, hear),—and ORO they could not under any con— sideration agree to. But if the basis of the c)ntribtrion was taken on the tonnage received at the ports there could be no possible grumb'ing on one side or the other. The port receiving the greatest benefit would have to make the largest contribution. That was the view the Health Committee took. . . .

Councillor SMETHURST was pleased to know that the committee had talon up the question of work whistles, and nothing. pleased him bett-.r tan to see a largo num- ber of people accepting the arrangement. He wished them to understand that the time limit would be taken notice of. He had not yet, however, heard one which had stopped at ten seconds. The time had been 21 and 23 seconds. He hoped that the works wc,uld keep to the arrangement. Councillor BENNETT asked whether they had power under an Act to control them ? The MAYOR : Yes.

Councillor BENNETT thought they ought to U9O it. It was questiocable whether they should allow them even ten seconds. He personally would rather hear some of the old-fashioned bells than the unearthly noise of the modern substitutes.

Councillor WILKINSON: I wish we had twice at; many. (Hear, hear). The MAYOR: Order. The minutes were then agreed to. Paving and Sewerage Committee. The Work on Wilderspool Causeway. Proposed Photographic Record of Old Buildings.

Alderman HEaTorr, in moving the confi-mation of the minutes, said the sewer behind the property in Bridge- street had been constructed. The construction of a gangway on the wharf at Howley was nearly completed. Alderman HUTCHINSON seconded.

Councillor BENNETT asked if any steps were taken in case of alterations necessitating the pulling down of interesting old houses to keep a record of the houses. He was not so impracticable as to imagine that they could save the whole of these houses, but he thought they ought to be putted down with a certain ;Lmount ef verence• An old house, said to be the residence in Bridge-street of the Earl of Warrington, had been partially demolished. There was a particularly inter- esting portion, a room with an extremely interesting gallery, and he was surprised to find the other day that that had entirely disappeared. The improvements rendered demolition inevitable, but he hoped Mr. Longdin would have taken a photographic record and vvi uld see that any relics touad wore taken to the MUROUM.

Alderman FRANCOMB asked if it were not pissible to put a few more men on the work in Wilderspo3l-road. It would eventually be a very beautiful road, but the work was dragging along at a very slow rate. He hoped the committee would put three or four times the number of men on it and get it finished. Alderman BOLTON called attention to the fact that the footpath on one side was 19 feet, and on the other side only two. He thought the committee should make them equal._ _ .

-Tne DEPUTY-MAYOR: I should like more men on Latchford-road. (Laughter.) The MAYOR : I believe this discussion is out of order.

Alderman BOLTON: I think there is something paid here for Wilderspool-road, and we are in order. Councillor JOHN EVANS asked if the gullies in Padgate- lane were connected with the sewers, and if not, why not ? He had had numerous complaints from inhabitants about the flooded state of the lane afttr the recent heavy rain.

Councillor Motims, referring to the construction of the gangway at the wharf at Howley, said they were spend- ing a good deal of money on what was practically use- less. He had all along been against the expenditure of money on that wharf and he did not think the Corpora- tion would ever make the slightest use of it. Ile wished they maid have some assurance that they had 'finished spending. If they had he had nothing further to say. If they had something more to pay they ought to take the matter into serious consideration. Alderman HEATON said this unfortunate committei had a largo number of questions asked cf it. Some were in order and some were not. (Laughter.) That sort of thing had occurred for a long time pass. It was not his intention,in future, unless members gave notice, to answer all questions put. With regard to Councillor Evans's question, when that district was sewered it was not intended that they should take any surface water. It was not intended yet. Many members knew-why. The sewage had now to be pumped If building opera- tions continued they would hare to provide for the sur- face water into the broek. They could net deal with it in their sewers.

Councillor Evaws : The gullies are not connected? Councillor HEATON : They are not and were never inten&A to be. Continuing, he said that Howley wharf had been a very long piece of work, and as far as the wharf and staging were concerned this expenditure would complete it. At the time this was proposed it was thought that it would be useful. He hopod yet that it might be useful. (Hear, hear). He had not the slightest doubt it would be useful some day for some- thing they were not now expecting. With regard to Wilderspool Causeway, it was very easy to say "Get men." They could not be got. In Liverpool and Man- chester they were paying them is an hour for their tram car purposes, and they were only working eight hours a day. The MAYOR : For sett paving. Alderman BRAWN: Yes. I think the work at Wilder- spool isgoing on very well. Alderman BOLTON reminded the speaker of the foot- paths. Alderman HEATON said it was now just as it was before the improvement of the road was commenced, and people then walked on the other side. The footpath was more than three feet wide where there were houses. If members would only be patient they would have one of the finest roads in the country. With regard to Councillor Bennett he had hardly the patience to listen to him. (Laughter) They had had an example of his folly at Bridge Foot. (Hear, bear.) He did not think anyone had a right to squander the ratepayers' money by suggesting that old houses should be allowed to remain standing. Councillor BENNETT said his suggestion was 'hat a photographic record should be kept of them, and he honed the committee would see that it was done. The minutes were adopted.

Scholarships for Elementary School Boys.

At a meeting of the Museum, Library, Arts and Technical Instruction Committee it was resolved that the Finance Committee be asked to place a rate of ti in the £ for technical instruction on the estimates for 1901-2. The scheme for the Charles Middleton scholar- ship for boys attending elementary schools, which way published in Saturday's" Examiner," was read, and it was resolved that the scheme he adopted and that two junior and one senior Charles Middleton scholarships be offered this year. The Secretary reported that a gift of £5 per annum for three years had been offred by Mr F W. Monks for a local scholarship in art for girls, and it was resolved that the hearty thanks of the committee be given to Mr. Monks for his kind offer. The Secretary reported that the following County Council ochibitions had been gained by students of the institute, one in science. by F. Daintith (second year) ; two in art, by H. H. Dell and Ethel Mottram. The MAYOR, in moving the adoption of the minute., re-narked that the scheme for the Charles Middleton scholarships had received a great deal of attention on the part of the committee appointed to consider it with the assistance of the Town Clerk. Councillor MONKS seconded the motion, which was carried*

The Fire Brigade Complimented.

Counchlor SMETHURST, in m wing the adoption of the minires of the Fire Engine Clrnmilee. said considerable work had been done by the Fire Brigade during the last month, and he thought they 6eserved every cen.gratula- tion upon the way in which tney had performed their duties. It was not often the firemen received much credit, but they had won great praise for so ably cop- ing with the ricent fires. For instance, they would have noticed the fire at Mr. Isher4ood's stables, adjeining which was a tannery containing a very valuable stock of leather, and the energies put forward by the firemen saved that building when people thought it was utterly impossible. It was a noted fact that every fire—they they had had four in one month—the Brigade had had to deal with, the men had succ-eded in keep- ing the conflagration within the building ic which it started. The amount of damage done at the three fires, excepting that at CockhAg,e gills, which was very heavy, was £1,809, and he might say that the superin- tendent had received a very pleasing letter from one of those places. It read as follows :—"Chapel Elon:e Farm, Great Sankey. I beg you to accept my warmest thanks for the assistance you rendered me on Monday last. The promptitude with which you arrived at the tire, and the untirtng effarts of the Bri.gaile.ceunlel with tne fact that one stack in a most dang-nrous position was ex:lnlet dy saved,was most praiseworthy. The fact that the Brigade had two ether fires to contend with practically the same day makes me feel they are entitled to every compliment far the excellent way in which they coped with toe fire. Yours respectfully, Jomna WRIGHT." Councillor PLIMPTON seconded, and the minutes were passed.

FLO NER AND BIRD SHOW AT

PEN KETH.

Fine Weather and a Record Entry.

Fine weather favoured the seventh anneal exhibitbri in connection with the Sink 'y and Penketh Cottage Garden and Bird Society which was held on Slturday afternoon. There was a record numbcr of entries in coo sequence of which, the show, which in former years has taken place in the schoolroom, was held in two msr- quees in the field opposite the Post Offi te, Penketh. the accommodation at the schools being iladequate. Much of the success of the event was due to the indefatigable eTtorts of the hon. secretaries. Mr G Clarke and Mr E R Godwin, to whom the greatest credi; is due. The attendance of the public was much larger than Usual, and the show was opened at three o'clock by the President of the society. Mr. Aitkin, who expressed his pleasure and sati4action az the increased support the committee had received from friends in the district, who had not only contributed in a monetary sense, but had also assisted by the excellent exhibits which hid added to the beauty of the show. That beiog the fist year in which the committee had attempted to hold the show in tents, it was a source of much thankfulness that the day was so beautifully fine. There was much in the show that was beautiful, some things that were good and some things that were excellent. He had much pleasure in declaring the show open. (Applause.) The fiswer, fruit and vegetable exhibits were tast,.fully arr4nged in the larger marquee, and in the centre was lsrge netnber of choice plants. .te., exhibited by Mr. Aitken, Mr. R. Garnett, and Miss Bolton. The eeneral quality of the exhibits was admitted to be excellent notwthstauding the lateness of the season and the effect of the recent weather. The judges were—garden section, Mr. Smith-Varley; bread, Mrs Photon; birds, Mr F W Heaton. The window plants were greatly admired, especially those in class five,which were in splendld con- dition. The first prize in that class was taken by Mr. E R Godwin. and the second and third wens to Mr. J. Davie a and Mrs Datton. There was not a large cellectioe of cut flowers. Most of the prizes were a,warded to Messrs. W Dutton (eight), S Moss (five), F Davies (four); and J Carrington (four). The bouquets of wild flowers made up by children under thirteen years of age,:eflectod much credit on the youngsters who had exercised great care in the arrange:nent of the blooms. In the vegetable classes there was a good entry. The potatoes peas and 'narrows shown were of partitalarly good quality and were a credit to the exhibitors. Mr. W Hill was success ful in winning the two first prizes for red and white Potatoes. In the fruit classes there was a poor entry.— Special prizes were given by the President (Mr B Aitken), Miss Bolton, Messrs Het-beets, Garland, Lawless, Podmore,Mather,Carter and Son,Owen and Sons,Righta Eustanne (Warrington), Baker (Peeketh). A. Cooper (Widnes), Cawley (Runcorn), and Hyde, and there was keen competition for them.

In the birds' department,some fine well-bred spscimens of both British and foreign birds were shown by the villagers. The best exhibits were adjudged to'be Mr. Borrill's nightingale. Mr. Jenkins' crest, and Mr. Peck's Yorkshire buff. The canaries were excellent birds and were highly praised. Mr. S: Booth. a well-known bird fancier in the village, exhibited two very uncommon birds—a waxwing and a popegardnel—both of which were in first CIR,EI condition.

_ The Penketh Tannery Bra sa Band played selections of music during the afternoon and evenincr. and Mr. R. Garnett opened his beautiful grounds to visitors to the show.

He knew he should receive perfectly unanimous support in that proposition, and he thought be might say without fear of contradiction that the society was most happy in its president. Be did not believe it would be possible to find a man from one end of the county to the other who had a warmer, personal and more active interest in an agricultural society than Gilbert Greenall had in that. When comparing the posi- tion of the society to-day with what it was eleven years ago, it was almost impossible to overrate the advantage it had obtained by Sir Gilbrt Greenall's connection with it. Be was not a purely honorary president, but be was acLively associated with it in every depirtment. (Hear.hear.) He would like to say,as he had done before, that if it paid farmers to breed good horses it certainly paid manufacturers and merchants to buy and use them. Be wished again to point out the great influence of the society upon the district. When they came to compare the animals—particularly those in the gelding class—with those of even a year ago, they realised the tremendous amount qf good brought about in the district tkrough tit, influence of the society. He hoped the society would benefit by having the in estimable advantage of having Sir Gilbert for its con tinuous president for years to come. (Applause.) The toast was drunk with musical honours,

WATER POLO.

NORM v. MIDLANDS.

This international test match was played at Walsall Baths before a large attendance. Neither aide was at full strength. The North started from the shallow end and showed much smartness, beating the Midland backs repeatedly. At half-time North were leading by three goals to one, and again having all the best of matters in the second half won easily by seven coals to three. For the winners Roney, Towers and Hodson each scored two go'ils and Wilkinson one, whilst Lirkin,Rounds and Taylor got three for the Midlands.

WARRINGTON v. WIGAN.

There was a crowded attendance at the Warrington Corporation Baths, Legh-street, on Tuesday evening to witness this r.turn match in connoction with the Liverpool and D:strict League. It will be remembered that in the first engag;ement. at Wigan, Warrington were defeated by three goals to nil. Warrington were poorly represented on Tuesday evening,the following being the home team :—A. G Robertson (goal), W Ashton, Baker and Sutton (forwards), Lowe and Warren (half backs). The following team did duty for Wigan :—Smith (goal), Saunders, Swift, and Matthews (backs), Elaselton, McGraw, and Nevinson (forwards). During the first half Wigan defended the deep end. From the throw in, Warrington got possession and play waged in the visitors' quarters until Saunders, of Wigan, broke away and sent in a good shot, which Robertson, the home goal keeper, warded off in fine style. Fouls were frequent. Ashton, of Warrington was heartily applauded for aishot which the Wigan custodian saved. At half thin ncnthAr side had scored. On chang- ing ends Wigan played up vigorously and Saunders scored two splendid goals, Robertson having no chance to save. Baker of Warrington, made two good attempts to score but his shots were weak and the final arrived vi ith the score Wigan two goals, Warrington nil.

ST. HELENS v. HYDE SEAL.

The return water polo match between St. Helens and Hyde Seal took place at Hyde on Tuesday night, before a good attendance, when St. Helens had revenge for the five goals to three defeat they received in the initial match at St. Helens. Final result : St. Helens 1 goals, Hyde Seal 2 goals.

LANCASTER v Sr. HELENS.

At Lancaster. The visitors were over an hour late. Lancast:!r were minus Kelly and Armitstead, and St. Helens were without Bretton and Walker. Lancaster though having the shallow end in their favour, were outclassed, the visitmrs scoring frequently. Mavisty opsned the score,and Preston added two goals. Robin- son scored for Lancaster, and Preston carried the visitors further ahead. Half-time: St. Helens, 4 goals; Lancaster, 1. In the second half, Kearnsley (two), Preston, and Green scored for the visitors. Result—St. Helens, 8 goals ; L incaster, 1. 4 two lengths squadron race was also decided, which Sc. Helens won by four yards.

Semi- final: Winner of district 1 v winner of district 2; winner of district 3 v winner of district 4.

Tke Bedstead Combination, At a meeting of the Bedstead Manufacturers Asociation, held at Birmingham, on Monday,it was ported that a nurn ber of firms had lately joined the secedere from the combination. The oustide houses are now in about the proportion of 16 to 35. Amongst those who have witherawn are some of the most important makers in the trade. The asso- ciation has been in existence about eight years. Operatives have been in alliance with the manufac- turers under an agreement to work only for associ- ated firms, the employers undertaking to maintain euce as lost their occupation by adhesion to this pledge. As the number of seceders has increased the drain on the funds for the support of men out of work, has grown to impossible dimensions, and lately a modification of the arrangement, was agreed to by the operatives Later seces- sions have threatened the combination with total disintegration, however, and at Monday's meet- ing it was recognised that, in the general interests of the trade, some reconstruction on lines accept- able to the revolted firms was highly desirable. The tzade is now in a very depressed condition, and there was a feeling that if step were not taken to avoid a war of 'low prices the effect weuld be dis- astrous to the industry. It was resolved to con- vene a general meeting of the trade to which representatives from all the bedstead manu- facturing centres of the kingdom should be invited, and that, in the meantime, no restrictions be imposed on associated manu- lecturers in regard to prices either for the home or export trade. During the existeixe... of the association, prices have advanced by 40 to -50 per cent., and operatives wages have been improved by 40 per cent.. It Is pointed out, however, in relation to selling prices, that the cost of all materials used in the trade has risen greatly in the same period. The independent action of seceders has caused a good deal of friction during the last year or two. The combination to sustain prices has also been bitterly resented by buyers, and many threats of reprisals have been made. E'XOELSIOR CYCLES at £lO Ka. Sole Agent, J. S. GARLAYD,S2, 53, and 55, Bridge-street,Warrington. Advt.

WARRINGTON AMBULANCE CORPS.

Annual Inspection at Walton Lea.

The pleasantly situated grounds of Councillor John Crosfield, J.P.. at Walton Lea, were, on Saturday afternoon the scene of an interesting event—the annual inspection of the Warrington Corps of the St. John Ambulance Brigade. Coun- cillor Crosfield has always evinced much interest in the ambulance movement, and this was demonstra- ted on Saturday when his beautiful demesne was placed at the disposal of the corps, The ambulance men assembled at their headquarters, the Gymnasium, Palmyra Square, at halt-past two and proceeded in 'buses to Walton. On arrival at Mr Crosfield's residence, they at once marched to a field. at the rear of the mansion, where the important event took place. fhere were on parade 24 privates and four supernumeraries, together with Captain Waring (chief superintendent), Superintendent Baldry, 2nd officer T J Hulse and 3rd officer F Taylor._

ihe Inspecting Officer was Mr. J. C. Derham, (Chief Constable of Blackpool), who is an assistant commissioner in number four district. The men were put through the various movements, which included stretcher drill, carrying wounded,infantry company drill, application ot bandages to wounds and putting up fractures,by Superintendent Baldry. At the close of the inspection, which was Witnessed by only a small number of the public, The INsPißdTon addressed a few remarks to the men. He said he was pleased with what he had seen alphough there was plenty of room for improve- ment. The stretcher drill was fairly good but was not sufficiently smart. It was carried out accurately but a little bit too slowly. With regard to the bandaging that also was done elowly, and they must do it a bit quicker. What he saw with reference to the putting up of a fractured thigh, considering the material they had at their disposal, was very well done. It behoved them as ambulance men to become efficient so that when they were called upon to deal with accidents they would be able to do so in a proper manner. The only way in which they could become efficient was by having frequent drills and practices. They should also read the in- struction books and take advantage of the doctor's lectures. If he had the honour and pleasure of visiting them again next year he hoped to find them more efficient. The inspection then concluded and the men were free to spend the remainder of the afternoon according to their inclinations. Most of the visitors availed themselves of the opportunity of inspecting Mr. Crosfield's grounds which are neatly laid out and trimly kept: The Walton Band was in attend- ance and discoursed lively music. Shortly after six o'clock a capital tea was partaken of in a marquee,the catering of Mr. Woodcock giving every satisfaction. At the conclusion of the meal Captain Waring called upon the Rev. L. J. T. Darwall, Vicar. of Walton, to present first aid and nursing certifi- cates and medallions to °number of members of the corps. Before performing this duty the Rev. L. J. T. DARWALL delivered a brief speech. He said it was a great pleasure to him to be present that after- noon, and he felt Captain Waring had done him an honour in asking him to distribute the certificates. He was sure they would agree with him that the- ambulance movement was of very great importance and was a movement which would spread every year. They felt the influence of it in their country parishes, and during the last few years he had. done what he could to foster a growth of ambulance feeling in Walton. During the three years preced- ing the present one they had a very good class, and. did their best to inspire a feeling of regard and enthusiasm for the work amongst their village men. At Walton they had very little opportunity of carrying out in practice the precepts they learnt. They should never forget the fact that the War- rington corps bad sent men to the front. In War- rington it was a great advantage that they had the opportunity of putting into prac- tice what they were taught. But, if they did not have this advantage in the country he thought the same enthusiasm prevailed. Ambulance men should feel proud to be the means of rendering help to others. The speaker quoted the old saying that"knowledge is poweeand stated that the saying came more true every day. in ambulance work especially it was very true. He thought they would feel that an inspection of that character was extremely useful and they should be strengthened and encouraged by it. In concluiol2 ha congratulated them upon having secured such a fine day.

'The Certificates &c. were then presented. The following is a list of the winners :—First aid certi- ficates : Privates Henry Maddock, William Webb, George Watkins, John Mahon, George Critchlay, Thomas Broomhall, Peter Rigby, James Wrench, Arthui: Horne, Harold Alcock ; medallions, Jesse Webb, ifenry Taylor; vouchers, William Paskin, William Kershaw; nursing certificates, Chief Super- int.:ndent W Waring, Superintendent S Bal- dry, 12nd officer T J Hulse, 3rd officer F Taylor; supernumerades, W D Fell, T Langley, W Newns, Privates J Kirby, J Fogg, T Davies, W Massey, T Maddock. J fl Pernberton,S Drinkwater,W Roberts, J W MEldleton, H Galloway, J Hill, J Harrison, Gilbert, J T Wilson, II Slater, T Miller, H Ilepple, McGowan, J Wilding, W Higham, H C Manning. Ti;e Rev. LJ T DARWALL read the following letter from Mr. R Pierpoint M.P,, to Mr. Hulse, " I have signed St. John Ambulance certificates and have pleasure in returning them to you. lam very glad that so many have earned certificates." 1 Attendance priz3s, which had been subscribed for by Captain Waring, Mr W B Roberts and Dr, ' Markel were presented to Superintendent Baldry, Superintendent Newns, Private Kirby, Superinten— dent Banning and Private Maddock. , Captain WARING proposed a vote of thanks to the Rev L J T barvvall for distributing the certificates and prizes.

Dar. HULSE seconded and said they all heartily appreciated Mr. Darwall's kindness. Ambulance work was for the good of their fellowmen and was aR good a work as anyone could undertake. He hoped that at the next inspection they would show therneelveg smarter men.

The motion was carried and Mr. DARWALL res— ponded.. ___

- Captain WARING proposed a vote of thanks to Councillor Crostield for the use of the field. He had been a very Rood friend to ambulance work and without the Crosfield family there would not have been much ambulanca work in Warrington. (Hear, hear.)

Superintendent BALDRY seconded, and referred to the good work done by the Crosfield family. They bad a good friend in Mr. George Cros- field, and they all wished him a hearty return from South Africa. When he did come back there was no doubt that he would take up his work ot loi:kinQ after and coaching the ambulance class. He (Mr Baliiry) did not think anything would givc, him mo'e pleaQure than to know that the Corps was beiry.; entertained 01 his father's ground. The vote was heartily carried and the proceed- ings terminated.

Several selections were given by the band and the remainder of the evening was agreeably spent. Anologies for lion-attendance were received from Mr .T Bridgwater (Manchester),Councillors Tinnion, Monk, and Greening, Misses Rimm3r, Miss Milner (Moore llow,e), Mr Fairhurst (borough treasurer),. and Mr..) Kinsley (gymnasium instructor).

THERE'S NOTHING HALF SO SWEET IN LIFE as LOVE'S YOUNG DREAM. So sang Tom Moore years ago, and everyone who has been fortunate enough to consummate a happy engagement by a suitable marriage will doubtless cordially endorse his statement. What could be pleasanter than to be the proud posssessor of a nice home, presided over by a loving winsome wife, whose sole desire is to made things comfortable for you and the little ones—when they arrive? Especially do you realise the charm when you know that thanks to your own industry and the economical methods of the aforesaid good lady the "nest egg "at the bank is steadily augmenting. Agreeable home surronedings, of course, tend to heighten the felicity whilst upholding the family dignity. Good furnishings. artistically and substantially constructed and tastefully arranged, not only give pleasure to all beholders but have an educational, an elevating influence upon the children, creates in their susceptible minds the desire to possess a similarly furnished home some day, supplied by the MIRO good old firm their rarents patronised, makes them frugal and thrifty, so that when they reach manhood or woman- hood other good orders are given to PITTS'S, of LONDON- ROAD AND GAYTH URN, who fulfil them in a satisfactory manner, as usual.

RESULT Ok'

SPRING CLEANING COMPETITION.

We have received such a number of really good parodies on Kipling's celebrated verses that we should like very much to give the prize to several dozens of them. As that, how- ever, is impossible, we hive had to make a choice and have decided upon which we think Is really the most appropriate. We publish this one, and also a selection of the others, not adjudging them the best of those which have been sent in but as a representative selection. We desire to thank all those who have taken the trouble to send us contributions towards this competition.

.23 was AWARDED to EDWARD H. DAVIS, Harborne, the following_ verses :—BOOTb

AbVlOl4 to HOUSEWIVES.

When you're started with Spring Cleaning—and you want

to do it clean

(And you know you cannot do it with your mouth)— Will you kindly buy a bottle of our Yurnitewrer Cream, Which is used in East and West and North and South. It's a powerful kind of compound, and it's very good to And you and Jane can take it as you find it. When it's used in active service, cleaning stains from " sticks,'' You will find it wont leave many marks behind it.

Boots shop, Boots shop, shop of a hundred things An enterprising Company, it's all the rage to-day • Everything sold for the lowest cash (as you can see for yourselves). THE EXA. MIN El? ,

AUGUST 17TH 1900.

THE Chinese situation Chinese Development. developes slowly; that, perhaps, is quite in keep- ing with the character of the Eastern race with whom we have to deal. The national anxiety has been much relieved since it became known that the British AMBASSADOR and his colleagues had not met the fate of the German MINISTER, but were " safe, if not saved." The successes of the allies at Tientsin • have evidently had their influence with the Chinese Government. An edict has been issued authorising the AMBASSADORS to telegraph in cypher to their respective Govern- ments. Several such messages have been received during the past few days. The fact that these have been permitted, and the issue of the edict seem to prove that the Imperial authorities at Pekin were responsible for the previous stoppage of communications, and to stamp upon them complicity in the international crimes that have been perpetrated. It looks clearer than ever that, whoever the men may be now dominating the Chinese capital, they have simply been "bluffing," the Powers. Recent events have, however, brought them to a sense of the peril in which they stand by their defiance of the civilised world and disregard of the comity of nations. It is reported that another Imperial edict has been issued appointing Li HUNG CHANG negotiator, or mediator, to make peace with the Powers. That wily and not over-scrupulous diplomatist has held office of this kind before. It was he who negotiated peace with Japan after the war. But he will probably find the offended Western Powers more difficult subjects to pro- pitiate than the simple "Japs," and more particu- larly as he has himself been regarded as a just object of suspicion throughout the whole course of the recent grave events. Some of the cypher despatches that the Cninese have permitted to come through intimate that the authorities at Pekin have been pressing the Ambassadors to withdraw to Tientsin, and trying t 3 persuade them that this is the wish of their own Govern- ments. But the Ambassadors cannot trust them- selves to the tender mercies of the Chinese, and will wait at Pekin until relieved by the allied forces. The immediate object, therefore, is still to press forward to Pekin as quickly as possible. The question of a Commander-in-Chief of the Pekin expedition has been brought to a rather abrupt issue by the Emperor WILLIAM, who, with the concurrence of Russia, Austria and Italy, has taken the matter into his own hands, and appointed Field Marshal Count von WALDERSEE to that posi- tion. The assent of Great Britain, France and America will possibly be given, if only to avoid any appearance of discord among the Powers in what ought to be joint etion undertaken in the interests of common humanity. It will, however, be something like six weeks before Count WALDERSEE can reach Tientsin, and meantime, it may be hoped, the Legations will be relieved by the force now on the way to Pekin. There does not appear to be any objection to Count WALDERSEE personally,though there may be exception taken to the matter of his appointment. But this is a minor matter com- pared with the more important one of future policy. If Great Britain can only induce the Powers to follow the lines of settlement laid down by Mr. BRODRICK when questioned in Parliament —no partition of territory, but the establishment of a strong Chinese Government, guaranteeing protection to foreigners and the "open door " even a German Field Marshal as commander may be temporarily tolerated.

THE recent rise in the The Great Coal price of coal has been Question. troubling many people be- sides the British Paterfamilias and his good matron. At all the recant meetings of railway companies the directors assigned the high price of fuel as a main cause of the falling off in dividends. Nobody professes to have much sympathy for the railway shareholder, especially if he be also a director. He is looked upon—we hardly know why—as a par- ticularly objectionable form of the "bloated capitalist." But when it becomes apparent that the entire railway enterprise of this kingdom yields less than 31 per cent on the capital expended, those who have money to invest very reasonably ask whether, after all, it is worth while making more railways. The travelling public, who are still demanding further railway facilities, would undoubtedly suffer if this idea began to fructify in the minds of investors. The falling off of some- thing like half a million in the amount distributed by the railway companies in dividends for the past half year is not wholly due to the rise in the coal bill; but that is the largest item in the in- creased expenditure which has occasioned it. The high price of coal is also the main factor in the apparent increase in our exports which the Board of Trade returns have shewn for some months past. The question has arisen whether the increase of 30 to 50 per cent. in the price of certain qualities of coal during the past twelve months is warranted by any failure in the supply, or whether it is only a rig of the market by the coal masters. There is no doubt that we have been drawing very heavily on our national stores of fuel, and ominous rumours of the exhaustion of coal-fields are being heard again. This subject is to be discussed by the savants of the British Association when it meets at Bradford next month. There is a growing feel- ing in certain quarters that the Government should L

impose an export duty on coal, of prohibit the exportation of steam coal for the sake of our Navy, or should do something to prevent our coal cellar running bare too soon. But none of these measures would reduce the price —rather enhance it, and dear coal might become almost as great a hardship as dear bread. Some of the railway magnates, however, are of opinion that the coal proprietors are driving too hard bargain with them and the public just now ; and that by refraining from buying [at present, prices will soon come down again. So the Midland Rail- way Company is only placing contracts for als months, and a number of steel works in York- shire have had a week's stoppage, simply to avoid having to purchase fuel at present prices. It might be regarded as another sign of a change la the seasons if coal should come to its "lowest sum- mer prices "at Christmas time. There seems to be that expectation in some quarters. In London householders have been recommended to hold their orders back awhile. We place this advice on record without expressing an opinion of its value.

The agenda for the monthly meeting of the War" rington Town Council on Tuesday evening bore on thelace of it distinct evidence of the growing ler portanee of the Corporation as a municipal body. On one page alone there was a record of the pees' ing in the last session of Parliament of two Bills materially affecting the town. One relates to the passing of the Tramways Order which will enable Warrington to draw abreast of the most progre.s- sive of municipalities in the provision of electric tramways and the other confers upon the town the more dignified title of County Borough, which carries with it additional cowers likely to become more and more onerous in the near future. OP October Ist next, the Corporation is to celebrate. the dawn of this new era in its municipal life anal all well-wishers of the town will hope that itS career as a County Borough may be in every NOY worthy of that dignified position. With regard to the tramways order it is some satisfaction 0 know that the provisions of the Tramway Act re' quire the tramways to be commenced within twelve months and completed in two years. This should have the effect of spurring on the Corporation 0 enter upon their scheme at once. It is too uuleh to expect that within two years such a large scheme of tramways will be carried out within the time stated but if we are able to see one section 30 use by that time we shall have no reason to conr plain.

The Labour Department of the Board of Trade reports some slight falling off in several of the more important industries, though noting that, on the whole,einployment at the end of the month remained fairly good, while wages continued to be at least slightly on the increase. Naturally, coal mining is mentioned as one of the industries in Which employment has remained good, and we cannot help adding an unofficial wish that this state of things may soon result in so obvious an excess of supply over demand as will take away the last excuse for the maintenance of famine prices. There is a falling off in the iron and steel manufacture, but this appears to be solely due to the temporary closing of works, either for the local holidays or on account of the extreme heat of the weather. A less favourable report is given of the tinplate trade, a considerable number of mills having stopped work. A decline in employment in the boot and shoe trade may be due to the fine weather, and a Speedy recovery after the holidays is to be looked for. The falling oft in the cotton trade is, how- ever, a more serious matter. Employment shews a great drop from the figures registered last year, and here, unfortunately, deeper and more per- manent cases of depression are to be traced. The Indian famine cannot but have great effect on the export trade, and to this as a disturbing influence the imbroglio in China may have to be added. On the whole the reports, however, give a favourable impression. There is, at all events, no definite indication of the setting in of that downward current which pessimistic prophets have for some weeks been foretelling.

What has now become known as the Thelwall nuisance is still very much to the fore. The Run- corn Rural District Councillors were engaged for some considerable time at Dutton on Tuesday in once more considering this vexed question. Long letters were read from both parties affected and anyone who cares to peruse the letters will find them interestingreading.Mr R A Naylor,who raised the complaint in the first instance, returns to the attack. He considers the nuisance serious enough " to bring disease, death, and desolation" in its train, and attributes it to the "vile matter" sent down the brook from the tanyard of Messrs. William Long and Sons. On the other hand the firm "unhesitatingly deny" that the tanyard effluent has ever been injurious to health and submit copies of analyses which they contend bear out their contention that the nuisance complained of is due to the in- sanitary arrangements existing on Mr. Naylor's property. Between the two parties it is difficult to decide, but it seems to us a case of the pot calling the kettle black, and the best way to re- move any complaints in the future is for both parties to take steps to prevent any further pollu- tion of the brook.

It is to be regretted that the inclement weather of last Thursday militated against the success of the annual show promoted by the Warrington and District Agricultural Society. The show itself Was the best which has been held in the district, and all that was wanted to complete the success of the event was a large gathering of spectators from the town and environs. This, however, was lack- ing and the small attendance may be attributed to the rain which fell intermittently throughout the afternoon. The turf squelched water at every step and it was difficult for those who showed their courage and endurance by braving the ele- ments to inspect the show with any degree of comfort or even comply with the popular request to "Listen to the Band.' As an exhibition of things agricultural the show was full of interest and almost every class showed a decided improvement Upon last year's exhibits. There are many evidences that the society is exerting a great influence in improving the breed of horses and cattle in the district. The show has already gained a high reputation and with such excellent officials as the society possesses, there is every reason to hope that the Warrington show will in future years be regarded as one of:the leading shows in the North of England.

The action of the London and North-Western "Railway Company is peculiar at times. The most recent example of this is afforded by the company's treatment of local anglers. For many years all anglers possessing the Provincial Angling Associa- tion's travelling card have been favoured with re- duced fares when proceeding on fishing expeditions. The privilege is enjoyed by anglers over all the rail- way systems of the country ,but for some reason or other, the London and North-Western Railway Company has withdrayvn —this —concession from -Warrington Anglers within the last few weeks, and as far as can be learned, -Warrington is the only centre which has been picked out for this arbitrary treatment. No explanation has been offered for the change and the Warrington Anglers are at a loss to know the cause. Surely it cannot be that the company's action is in the form of a reprisal against Warrington in consequence of the action of the Corporation with reference to the tramway order! We hope not, for the credit of the railway company.
