MATTERS OF MOMENT.

A Provincial Letter.

So long as the short service system is part and parcel of the constitution of the British Army, it is reasonable to expect that employers of labour throughout the country will view with favour the appeal made by the Secretary for War to give em- ployment to Reservists. There is no desire to see the Army in England as it exists in France, but there is a growing wish that it shall be made more popular, and that the men shall receive proper en- couragement. Of course, no greater encouragement can be given than by employers showing a readiness to engage discharged soldiers and men of the Army Reserve. This readiness has not always been dis- played but recently a change has taken place in public feeling, of which there was evidence in abundance at the outbreak of the war with the Boers. Employers in all parts of the provinces undertook to keep open the situations of the reser- vists from the moment they were called to fight our battles in South Africa. Since then the occupa- tion of the s9ldier has continued to rise in the esti- mation of the public. But, as the Secretary for War reminds us,the discharged reservists cannot be put in the way of obtaining civil employment by the military authorities, and he must rely upon employment registries and the local organizations -which have been formed for the purpose of pro- moting the interests of the ex-soldiers. Appeals through the newspapers and the holding of public meetings are among the suggestions put forward ior the purpose of meeting the case of Reservists returning from the front.

On the principle of going " from " home to hear the news, one has to go to the "Man of Mark Lane" for the information that quite a scare has been caused in the Fylde district in consequence of rumours among the peasantry on the seaboard of Ireland that the French were about to land troops in Erin's isle. The appearance of British warships off the coast during the naval manceuvrs, says the writer, was accepted as proof of the rumour' and letters were received by several of the Irish har- vesters in the Flyde district of Lancashire,advising them to return home. These were passed from hand to hand, and a state of panic arose. During the course of a single night more than 200 Irishmen left one district alone, and it has been ascertained that the majority booked for Dublin. It is esti- mated that more than a thousand labourers thus disappeared without warning. Of course,somebody had a good laugh, but if half what the "Man of Aark Lane" flays were true, it would be no laugh- ing matter for the men, any more than for their employers. -

There could be no valid objection to the Railway Companies exacting their full due, or "pound o-f flesh," in regard to excess luggage, if they were to provide a properly organised staff for adequately coping with the extra work which must necessarily arise. But, if a correspondent in the " limes" is correct, this has not beeu done; nor do the Com- panies appear to have any intention of doing it. It Is understood that charges for excess of luggage are to be made whenever the present staff of officials can 'and time to attend to the work of weighing. it fol- lows that if the matter is left to chance in this way Charges will be made at some stations and not at -ethers, and one passenger will be charged, while

another equally liable will go scot free. Indeed, according to one correspondent's statement he was charged two shillings on his way to the seaside, while on the return journey no charge was made. The public do not care to be treated in this hap- hazard way, and are right in complaining as they do. So that the companies should really make up their minds to either make the charges and provide an adequate staff and machinery for doing the work, or drop the charges forthwith. But the probable secret of the matter is that the revenue derived from charges on excess luggage would not be sufficient to make it profitable for the companies to provide an adequate organisation; while by trusting to the charges being le vicid by the existing staffs, wherever opportunity offers, they see the chances of an augmented revenue without any corresponding expense.

As the time approaches for the holding of the Brewster Sessions, there are indications of the adoption of a new policy at the Court to be held in Liverpool for the renewal of licenses. Owing to the demolition of insanitary properties and other causes the authorities believe that in various dis- tricts of the city there is a great excess of licensed houses, and an intimation has been issued to the trade that so far as the areas in question are concerned, the magistrates will, in deal- ing with the renewal of licenses, take into consideration solely the wants ot the neigh- bourhood. The application of this principle will no doubt result in a reduction of the number of public houses,and P.,. is expected that thequestion will be strenuously debated by " the trade," who have been invited by the magistrates to voluntarily surrender licenses. The matter is of interest to other towns and citiss as well as Liverpool; and in Birmingham the ccturse mentioned has been readily adopted by the brewers for some years in succes- sion. The difficulty, of course, is with the private owner, who, while giving nothing himself, benefits by the licenses surrendered by others.

The bathing season has produced a number of accidents,of course; it always does. But there has hiTherto been an absence of the newspaper head- line, the" Drowning Season," owing let us trust,to the fatalities being fewer or of a less sensational character than in some seasons. As it is, however, many lives have been lost. It goes without saying that many lives would be spared every year if only ordinary care were taken. For instance, some per- sons are in the habit when at the seaside of going off alone to bathe in some secluded place away from the ordinary bathing locality; while others who can swim a little, but not sufficiently to battle with strong currents, go out into waters of which they know little or nothing. A representative of each of these two classes has this week paid with his life for his folly in South Devon. One was overtaken by cramp; while the other was carried out by a strong current.

FRODSH AM.

THE ODDFELLOWV CLUB.-011 Sunday morning the members of the Frodshim Court Independent Order of Oddfellows attended Divine service at the Parish Church. There was a good muster. The Widnes Subscription Band was in attendance, and healed the procession during the march t ) and from the church. On its return the band played a number of selections. PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE.-91 the monthly meeting of the Parish Council, acting as Parochial Committee, held in the Court Room, on Friday evening, there were present—Messrs T Earlam (presiding), J M Williams, W E Shore,G Rodgers,P Booth,J W Rothwell,S Davies, G Gleave, W Youd, Ashton (clerk), Farrington (inspector), and Rodgers(water engineer).The Clerk read a letter from the Cheshire County Council intimating that the Council could not see their way to adopt the suggestion made by the Parochial Committee with regard to vehicles with two or more horses carrying an extra light. The Clerk reported that he had written to the Manchester Ship Canal Company complaining of the holding up of the water in the river Weaver, and a letter had been received from Mr. W. H Hunter asking for information. The report of the Water Committee was produced, from which it was shown that the Railway Company had applied for a supply of water for the stationmaster's I' house, and on the recommendation of the Water Com- mittee it was decided to charge the rate of Li per annum. The Inspector of Nuisances reported that his attention had been called to the emission of black smoke from the Jam Work 3 chimney, and it was r Fiolved that the Inspector write to Messrs Kydd and Kydd, Limited, calling their attention to the discharge of smoke, and making a suggestion which would, if followed out, abate to some extent the smoke nuisance.

GOLDEN WEDDING CELE- BRATION AT WARRINGTON.

On Tuesday evening, Mr. and Mrs. George Savage of Wellfield-street, Warrington, celebrated the anniversary of their 50th wedding day. The guests which included the Mayor (Alderman H. Roberts) and Mayoress, Councillor and Mrs Shaw Green, Mr and Mrs Hayward, Mr and Mrs R G Henshall, Mr and Mrs SG- Davey, Captain and Mrs W A Savage, Mr and Mrs Rees, Mr and Mrs McGookin, with many friends from a distance to the number of over 50 were received by Mr and Mrs Savage at the Winmarlegh Cafe, Sankey-street. Some charming presents had been presented to the venerable couple, and were displayed on the table in the upper room. A most excellent repast had been provided by the Directors of the Café Company at the conclusion of which the Mayor, in suitable terms, proposed the health of their host and hostess. In the course of a very chaste address he referred to the long time Mr. and Mrs- Savage had been associa- ted with Warrington, to his useful life in social work, to the foundation and conduct of the business of the Zillah Shipping and Carrying Company, Limi- ted, to his geniality and uprightness in all of which as the speaker said he had been helped and aided by his good wife, and he trusted that there might still be many years before them, but that when the time came as it must for them to part, their lives were such as to justify the assurance that it would be merely to enter upon a happier and more enduring state.

A most touching letter from Mr. Brouah referring to his personal acquaintance and knowledge of the host and hostess was then read in which he wished them continued happiness and prosperity. Mr Savage in responding said the kind remarks of the Mayor had almost deprived him of the power of speech. He came to Warrington 60 years ago a poor boy. He had been greatly blessed and pros- pered. He met his wife here and she had been a true helpmate. When he looked back upon his life he felt like the Queen of Sheba after seeing the greatness and glory of the court of King Solomon when she said" there was no more spirit left in her." When he remembered coming a poor boy and that now he was entertaining as his guests the Mayor and Mayoress, a Town Councillor, Justices of the Peace, bank managers &c.,he was overwhelmed with gratitude, and he desired on behalf of his wife and self to express to one and all their great thanks for their kindness in coming to greet them on this the anniversary of their fiftieth wedding day. At the conclusion of the speeches a very pleasant and enjoyable evening was spent.

PADGATE.

DEPARTURE OF AN OLD RESIDENT.—Miss Sansomsafter a neriod of over 23 years' residence has recently severed her connection with Padgate. Miss Sansom, who was held in high regard, was formerly headmistress at the Church schools, and since relinquishing these duties has conducted a private school in the village, which, owing to ill-health, sbe bas had to give up and remove to recruit her health to Ansdel, near Lytham. THE INSTITUTE. —The members spent a social evening on Friday evening, the occasion being the presentation of prizes won in the recent billiard handicap. Ahout 80 members and their wives sat down to tea, alter which an exhibition game was played between Messrs F Drink- water and J Taylor. A concert followed, items losing contributed by Messrs F Blackwell, J Ryder, Drink water. A Brindle and E Young. Seaga were also given by the Rev F E Evans (curate-in-charge of the parish) and Mrs Evans. During an interval in the programme the prizes were distributed by the Rev F E Evans, as follows :- Ist prize (an oil painting, presented by Mr W H Drink- water), Mr Joseph Davies; 21d. prize (shaving outfit), Mr James Roberts ; 3rd priz, (pipe in case, presented by Mr Joseph Roberts), Mr William Bowering ; 4th prize (amber cigarette holder in silver case), Mr Thomas Smith. A very enjoyable evening was spent, one of the leading features being contributed byMr Joseph Roberts on the gramaphone.

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AUGUST 17, 1900.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

THEATRE ROYAL RUNCORN.

The Runcorn magistrates on Monday held a special sessions for the purpose of hearing an application for a license to perform stage plays at the Theatre Royal. The magistrates on the Bench were Sir Richard M. Brooke Bart., Alessrs. G L Davies W Handley, F G Norman and S Taylor. Mr. W. Caddick, the lessee, made the application and stated he had been lessee of the theatre Uar eleven years. Sergeant Pearson had no objections to raise and the license was granted. A VIOLENT CUSTOMER.

At Widnes, on Thursday morning, Bartholomew Bowles was fined forty snillings and costs for being disorderly and refusing to quit the Stanley Arms, Widnes. The defendant is a notorious character, and owing to his conduct Mr Whittaker had given instructions that he must not be served in the house. On the 4th inst, he went there and caused a disturbance, and threw a big stone, which struck Mrs Whittaker,knocking some of her teeth Out. NELSON-STREET WESLEYAN SCHOOL.

The annual sermons in aid of the funds of this school, which performs an admirable work in the district where it is located were preached on Sun- day by the Rev Joseph Rosnett, of Leicester. The services were held in Camden Chapel and were well attended. Excellent singing by the choir and scholars was a pleasing feature. The collections realised £22 12s 01—a record for the school anni- versary. PRESENTATION AT RUNCORN.

The members of Mr.T Simister,' select class at the Brunswick Sunday school. on Friday last presented to Mr John I Simkin (one of their number, who was leaving for South Africa for the benefit of his health), with a very valuable gold mounted umbrella suitably engraved. —Mr Simkin returned his sincere thanks to the members of the class and promised Co treasure their present in remembrance of their spontaneous generosity.—The presentation was made by Messrs W F Jones bind Williamson.

A THOUGHTFUL ACTION BY A WIDNES BAND.

On returning from Frodsham on Sunday, where they had been attending an Odcittellowi' Church Parade, the Widnes Subscription Prize Band called at the house of James Harrison, Wivern-place, Run- corn, and played a number of nymns. Harrison is a confirmed invalid, and the band on several occa- sions has been instrumental in raising sums of money for the benefit of himself and his aged mother. Their kind and thoughtful action on Sun- day was much appreciated. RUNCORN CYCLE CARNIVAL. _ _

The Mystic Letters which have been placarded about for the last week or so, rendered into English ask the question" Who will help the Cottage Elos- pita! Fund ?" We venture to reply everyone, for truly there is no more deserving object. With their usual generosity the Runcorn Cycle Club officials and their friends have again instituted the Cycle Carnival,and on Wednesday next. we are prom- ised an interesting and delightful spectacle as well as an opportunity to contribute our mite. CHURCH LADS' BRIGADE. On Saturday afternoon about ten members of the Runcorn Company of the Church Lads Brigade re- turned from South Shore, Blackpool, where they have spent a week in camp, in company with a large number of other brigades forming the North- Western Division. They should have left Runcorn for camp on the preceding Saturday, but owing to the camping ground being flooded they had to go from Widnes early on Monday morning. The weather was rather wet in the early part of the week, but the lads made the most of the few fine days which favoured the proceedings. They were accompanied by the Rev. 11. B Firth and Mr. John Littler.

COMPENRATION CASE AT WIDNES.

A case came before his Honour Judge Goldthorpe on Saturday at the County Court, in which Charles Byrne, Caroline-street, sued the United Alkali Company, Limited, under the Workmen's Compen- sation Aet, for half wages owing to incapacitation from work. Mr. Knowles appeared for the plaintiff and explained that terms of settlement had been arrived at. The plaintiff was to receive £lO in liquidation of past and future payments. The costs were agreed at .E 7 14s 61. The facts of the case were briefly these :—On June Bth the plaintiff was loading cask 3of bleaching powder into a waggon by means of a crane. His fellow workman released his hold of his handle and the plaintiff's handle was twisted out of his hand, and he received a blow on the head which inflicted a wound. This prevented him from following his employment.

COOK-E AND THE ARAB.

Henry Cooke, a well-known Runcorn character, has appeared in many characters, and in all sorts of company before the Runcorn Bench, and in the majority of instances his appearance before his superiors has been but the prelude to a longer or shorter period of banishment to the house of cor- rection at linutsford. On Tuesday morning last he bawed before the Bench in company with Mrichand Ben Saadi, an Arab, to answer to a charge of drunkenness in Bridge-street. Cooke was also charged with begging.—No doubt "Knobby" who possesses a ready tongue would have prevailed upon the Bench that there was a mistake some- where, but the evidence of P.C.'s Wilson and Gregory was too much for him, and he got 14 days hard labour for each offence. —The Arab, however, was man enough to admit he had "too much wine," and got off with the payment of 53 fine. RUNCORN SONS OF TEMPERANCR.

On Saturday afternoon the juvenile members of the Runcorn Sons of Temperance, entrained at Runcorn Station,at about 2 30,0 n their annual trip to Overton. The weather was all that could be desired and consequently a good number made the journey. The hills were soon thronged with pleasure seekers enjoying themselves in various ways. The different amusements on the summit were all well patronized and the beautiful scenery round the hills also proved a source of attraction. During the evening a number of excellent races were witnessed, the successful competitors being presented with useful prizes. The return journey was made about 8.30 p.m., after a very successful trip The following officers. and friends were in charge of the children; --Niessrs. T R Whyte, patron; and Edward Key. jun., secretary; and Messrs J Johnson, T J Holt, E Bey, sen., T Clarke, T Dutton, and I) Bisbrown. The juvenile section is now in a very flourishing condition, numbering 123 members. _

ASSAULTED WITH A BAR OF IRON.

John Wickstead, firewood seller, Granville•street, appeared in Runcorn Police Court on Thursday morning with his head swathed in bandages, and preferred a charge of unlawful wounding against Lizzie Whelan, a woman with whom he cohabited. The charge, however, was reduced to a common assault and the ,circumstances, as narrated by wit- nesses to Messrs W Handley and S Taylor were as follow :—On Tuesday afternoon the prisoner was absent from home. In the evening at half- past seven she returned. She was under the influence, of drink and Wickstead, with more vigour than politeness inquired "Where have you been to, you drunken fool." Lizzie stayed not to bandy the words which break no bone., but seizing a bar of iron, aimed for Wickstead's bend, and struck him on the face near the left eye adding the definite assertion. " You will not call me a drunken fool again." Wickstead bled " like a pig" and remorse seized Lizzie's heart, and she humbly begged forgiveness, but Wickstead lacked that divine attribute, and P.C. Bratt was sent for and eventually that assiduous officer locked the woman up after rendering first aid. Wickstead was taken to Dr McDougall's surgery where Dr Bethune put three stitches in the lacerated wound which was an inch long and half an inch deep. Lizzie had a plausible explanation for the magistrates. There was, she said, a projecting nail in the door and she had twisted the bar over her shoulder in order to drive it into the wood when unfortunately Wickstead's face came in contact with the sharp end. It was quite an accident, for which she was very sorry. The magistrates.believed there was intent to strike Wickstead and not the nail, and for six weeks Lizzie will have the opportunity of ruminat- ing upon the perverseness of that iron bar, in the brief respite she may have from the bard labour of Knutsford prison.

THE THELWALL NUISANCE.

More Correspondence.

At the meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Council on Tuesday, Mr. Knowles presiding, further correspondence was read with reference to the above mentioned subject. Mr. R A Naylor, wrote as follows from the Angel and Royal Hotel,Doncaster,under date August Iltb. —"To G F Ashton, Esq., Runcorn, Dear Sir: The Tannery Nuisagce,Thelwall.—ln consequence of the unexpected turh of events I beg to draw the atten- tion of your Council to the following facts. (1) that your Council has not taken any steps to ascer- tain the real cause of thelgreat nuisance of which we have complained, the consequences of which have been of such an alarming character. (2) That the supposed iuspaction of the tanneries by your Committee was quite useless, in view of the fact that as soon as the gentlemen forming the Com- mittee were beyond the smeling distance, this horrible nuisance was very much in evidence. (3) , Your Medical Officer describes the effluent from the Tannery Brook "a very bad one," and as this was taken under very favourable conditions I beg to say that ypu might have obtained another sample at least ten times the strength of the one your Inspector submitted. (4) Your Inspector positively declined when taking the above sample to secure one from the two pipes com- plained of, stating that the discharge being almost pure water, this would be useless? Vbat about the house of some members of your Council and 200 or 300 more within your area it these drains can be rightly called a nuisance. (5) The second sample of the Tannery effluent was taken after being mixed with the discharge from the two pipes before men- tioned, and in your Medical Officer's opinion this diluted effluent was "the better of the two." This seems to prove that the supposed sewage from these two pipes improve the vile matter sent down the brook from the Tanyard. (6.) That this abominable smell from the Tanneries and the stench from the brook still continues, to the injury of Her Majesty's subjects, and is quite sufficient to bring disease, death and desolation as it has done in the past. All that I have before stated I shall be glad to prove, and can furnish your Council —if desired— with scientific evidence of the Most startling character." Messrs. W. Long and Son wrote to the Chairman of the Council under date August 13th, 1900 .—" Dear Sir, We have received through your inspector conies of two anal7ses by Mr Carter 4ell of our tanyard effi uant— )ne t ik an in Waste-lane and the other after its junction with the two sewer p.pes from the Thelwall side. So far as our effluent is concerned we are now engaged in experi- ments to see what can b 3 done to extract more of the solid matter, and you may rely on our doing everything in reason to render it as little offensive as can be. That it has ever been injurious to health we unhesitatingly deny and after the cruel charges that have been made through letters to your board and the public press we desire to call your attention to the enclosed analyses which were taken by Dr. Richmond of Warrington, at our request prior to your visit here on the 11th of July and suomitted by him to a competent analyst, prior also to any tampering with the drainage on the Thelwa'l side of the culvert, and when flowing in its normal condition. We submit his is sliewn to be Sufficient to account for the sickness and deaths of which we have hs•ari so much in the past, especially when, as we are told was the case, rain water down spouts were directly connected un-trapped with a ciwed sewer drain where gas must have been generated. We now leave the matter confidently in your hand to see that all who have offended against the laws of sanitation are shewn a better way."—The letter was accompanied by copies of the analyses referred to. In answer to Mr. Linaker, Mr. Farrington (the inspector), stated that he had served notices upon Messrs. Long and Naylor. No other notices had ben served by him as he was not aware other owners con- tributed to the nuisance complained of. He had received no replies. Mr. Linaker further asked whether Mr Farrington had inspected the premises of Mr Naylor; whether there was any nuisance arising from Mr. Naylor's property ; and what condition his drainage arrangements were in ? The Inspector stated that he thought the drainage arrangements were rather batter than others, but he had been unable to take a sample of the sewage,etc because at the time there was nothing flowing through the pipe. There was not sufficient flowing to go through a pencil holder. That was his reason for not taking the sample, and he had not refused as stated by Mr Naylor. Whether the flow had been stopped intentionally or not he could not say, but he had been told by Mr. Naylor's man that if he stopped for an hour it would come. Be could not, and did not wait Dr. Adams pointed out that there were numerous dis- crepencies in the various statements regarding the smal or no smell,and he suggested, if that was to be a reason for deferring the subject fortnight after fortnight that something should be done to set a continuous watch for twenty-four hours, a week, or even longer, and thus gave whether the matter did arise from the Tannery. Admiral Parry did not see what more was wanted. Dr. Adams had told them that it was detremental to the public health. Be had passed the place at ten o'clock at nicht and the smell had been enough to kill an ox. (L-ughter.) The Inspector further stated there was a certain time n which the parties were allowed to do what they would. He would attend to the matter and report in due course. Ihe Chairman That is all we can do. We had better go on with the next business. At a later stage of tbe proceedings Dr Adams said he had been trying to make comparisons between the analyses submitted by Messrs Long. and that of the County Analyst, but owing to the difference in terms he was unable to do so effectively, for he could not tel whether the same standards had been employed. There was, however, a great disparity between the two. Admiral Jones Parry added that he felt sure upon the point bnause he felt that their Inspector and Medical Officer were at fault. It commenced about six months ago, and unless it was very carefully handled it would incur a very great expenditure all round. Be (the Admiral) was of opinion that if the place was piped over with a good pipe drain with a valve to prevent the water returng at the end, they would bear no more about it. 'roe whole question had arisen because the Medical Officer in the first instance had refused to say it was injurious to health. He did not believe that the cases of diphtheria were due to it, but now they had something to go upon, they were told that Mr. Long's effluent was injurious to health. Be was not there to defend Mr. Naylor in any way, but. (turning to Mr Farrington) Mr Naylor had told Mr Farrington he was at liberty to do as he liked. Mr. Farrington passed no remark. Admiral Parry: Do you not hear my remarks Mr. Farrington. lam speaking on a subject of sanitation that should be interesting to you? Mr. Farrington: Certainly I hear all you say. Admiral Parry • Lid you say there was any nuisance; and did Mr. Naylor say you were at liberty to do any- thing you liked? Did he or did he not. Mr. Farrington: yes. - -

Mr. Linaker did not agree with Admiral Parry for it was his opinion that a nuisance could not be cured by piping in a ditch. It would have to be purified. Dr. Adams concurred and said if the pipe was laid there it would very soon be blocked and running all across the road. The sewage bad been condemned as unfit to go into any open stream. To d‘al with the mat- tPr thoroughly they would have to go to the purification of effluents: or the bed-rock of the Rivers Pollution Scheme.

Admiral Parry adied there was actually no sewage from Mr, Naylor's Cottagesor his house. The Chairman : Oh ! but we have seen it. Mr. S. Woodward said it was not sufficient for Mr. Naylor or Mr. Long to say to the Inspector you can do what you like. (Hear, hear.) It was for Mr. Long and Mr. Naylor Cu purify their effluents. (Hear, hear.) The Chairman Just so. We had better proceed with the next business. The matter then dropped.

Prize Distribution at Daresbury.

The scholars attending the Dardsbury Board Schools were on Monday premented with money prizes, &c., from the school endowment fund for regular and punctual attendance during the last six months The following governors were present— Rev II T Clark, and Messrs J Cooper and J Ockles- ton, of Runcorn. Prizes were, as usual, also given for marks received for different subjects during the school session, in addition to prizes for regularity of attendance. The following children have never missed a single attendance during the last six montbs,and in addition to the highest money prize, received a useful present or medal :—Nellie War- burton, John Taylor, John Jameson, Florence Buckley, William Jameson,Arthur Moulsdale, Emily Broome, Thomas Lee, John Boaler, Wilfrid Littler Ethel Prince, Maggie Littler, James Houghton, Frank Ryder, Garfield Furnival, William Jolley, Annie Ttylor, Alfred Warburton, Ethel Ryder, Cyril Furnival, James Ryder,Percy Wright,Arthur Ryder, Frank Lightfoot, Walter Prince, Florence Furnival, Elizabeth Littler, Herbert Leach, Ellen Endersby, and Hilda Endersby. After the distribution the children dispersed for the customary half-holiday.

THE ATTEMPTED SUICIDE AT PRESTON BROOK

A letter was read from Alfred Wright, who was recently before the Runcorn magistrates on a charge of attempting to commit suicide,thanking the matroo, the doctor, and the nurses for their kuld treatment of him during his conflaeaunt at the Work louse.

Mr.-Williams (die chairman) said he lived the press would notice the letter. The public generally were very ready to deprecate the treatment meted out in work- hous?s, and he thought the publication 'of this letter would d 3 something to wards removing any ials3 im- prssion which might exit.

IN LIEU OF OLD AGE PENSIONS. FOOD FOR REFLECTION.

A lengthy communication was read from the Local Government Board, dealing with the question of the provision of Cottage Homes and the better treatment of the deserving poor generally, in accordance with the recommendations of the Select Committee last year. Reference was made to the question asked recently in the House of Commons as to whether the Government would be prepared to introduce and carry through a bill on the lines suggested by the Select Com- mittee in the event of an assurance being given that the measure should be regarded as non- controversial. In his reply, it was pointed out that the President of the Local Government Board ex- pressed his desire to deal with the question, but found himself unable to do so. However, the Local Govern- ment Board had in contemplation a series of new regula- tions with a view to securing better accommodation and treatment for the aged and deserving poor. Were a bill to be introduced, it would require the aid of tbe Chancellor of the Exchequer, and having reg trd to the enermeus expenditure to be met at present, he had come reluctantly to the conclusion that this wes not the proper time to make further demands. The letter went on to mention some of the recommendations of the Select Committee, among them the removal of imbeciles from the workhouse, and also the removal of children. The latter was a question upon which public opinion was undoubtedly upon the side of the Select Committee, and Boards of Guardians should allow no opportu- niry to pass for carrying that suggestion into effect. Cottage homes ought; to provide opportunities for re- moving children from workhouse surroundings. With regard to the aged and deserving poor, it was felt that they should receive different treatment from those whose previous habits and characters had not been satisfactory. They should not be urged to enter the workhouse unless there was some cause which rendered sach a course necessary, such as infirmity of mind or body. Oat-door relief in such CaS93 should be adequate, and the Board are afraid that in such cases out relief is often inadequate in amount. The regulations in con- templation are as follow :- 1. That the Guardians should form a special class of inmates of 65 years of age and upwards with re- gard to whom the Guardians, after due inquiries' have satisfied themselves that by reason cot their moral character or behaviour or previous habits they are sufficiently deserving to be members of the class. 2. That for such inmates extra day roeine should be provided, which might, if thought desirable, be available for members of both sexes, in which they would have the opportunity of separation from disreputable inmates, and in which their meals, other than dinner might be served at hours fixed by the Guardians. 3. That sleeping accommodation in separate cubicles should be provided for them. 4. That privileges should be given them as regards the hours of going to bed and rising. 5. That considerably increased liberty should be granted to them, and greater facilities for being visited by their friends. 6. That for each inmate of this class a locker should be provided; the key would be retained by the inmate, but it would be required that the contents of the locker should be open to proper inspection; and 7. That as regards the inmates of this class the pro- visions in the orders relating to the supply of tobacco, dry tea, and sugar should be made compulsory. Boards of Guardians, the circular went on to state,are at present empowered to deal with most of these matters, and in some cases they have already done so. The difficulty with which many Boards of Guardians will have to contend in connection with the extra classifica- tion contemplated is the want of room, but it is hoped that where there is room the Gatardians will not hesitate to take steps to bring about improvements of the kind indicated in the arrangements for the aged and deserving poor. In some instances, as has been pointed out, the room required could be provided by the removal of the children from the workhouse, and the Board trust that no opportunity will be lost of adopting this course where it is practicable,and that the Guardians in all cases will give the whole subject their serious consideration with a view to improving the lot of the aged and deserving poor..

Owing to its importance the clmmunication was re- ferred for discussion at an early hour at the next meet- ing of the Board.

LICENSING OF HACKNEY CARRIAGES AND DRIVERS•

Mr. Reginald Potts, clerk to the County Coun- cil, wrote as follows under date 2nd August, 1900 : Dear sir: The Chief Constable of this County has re- cently suggested to one of the committees of this Council the advisability of regulations being made dealing with the licensing of hackney carriages and the drivers thereof in the Rural Districts of the county. The pro- visions of the Towns Police Clauses Act 1847 and 1889 with respect to hackney carriages by being incorporated in the Public Health Act 1875 (sec. 171) are in operation within urban districts and consequently the Councils of those districts are in a position to frame bye-laws and regulations for the licensing of hackney carriages and the drivers thereof. The Local Government Board have by section 278 of the Public Health Act, 1875 and section 25 (3) of the Local Government Board Act 1894 power by special order to apply to rural districts any of the powers exercised in urban districts under the Public Health or other Acts. My Committee have instructions to bring under the notice of your Rural District Council the suggestions of the Chief Constable with a view to their obtaining for their district the Order of the Local Government Board to put into operation therein the clauses in the Towns Police Clauses Acts with respect to hackney carriages and the drivers thereof. I shall be obliged if you will in due course bring this communica- tion before your Council, and inform me what action, if any, they will take thereon. ' On the proposition of Mr. Samuel Woodward,seconded by Mr J. Eiwards the letter was referred to the Parlia- mentary Committee.

THE WATER SUPPLY OF NORTON-.-LOONCING AHEAD.

Mr. H. W. Cook, clerk to the Wallasey Urban District Council, on August 13b, 1900, wrote as follows:—"Dear Sir, Liverpool Corporation and this Council, Water Sup- ply. In view of a possible appeal to the Local Govern- ment Board upon this Council's proposal to lay a water main from Vyrnay to the boundary of the Wallasey Dis- trict, and having regard to the fact that opposition to the scheme is contemplated by the West Cheshire Water Co. I shall be obliged if you, as clerk to the Runcorn District Council, will if you hive any objections to the proposal, forward those objections to the Local Govern- ment Board at an early date, so that no delay may arise injobtaining the decision of the Board as to the proposed works, Of course you will understand that Ido not forward this letter to invite opposition, but only assum- ing such opposition to be contemplated so that what is to be done shall be done quickly." The Clerk also read a letter from Colonel W. R. Lascelles, chairman of the Norley Parish Council who wrote !under date August 13;h, 1900, stating that he was much interested in the question of getting a water supply for that parish, and was aware that the matter had been before the District Council. Be saw in the local papers an advertisement for the Wallasey District Council from which it appeared that the Livernool Water Main was about to be tapped close to the Zion Chapel in the parish of Norley. He wished to urge the importance of the opportunity not being lost, and that when the pipe was tapped a junction should also be put in to the Norley side of the pipe, so that when the time came (as it surely would) for them to arrange for a supply for the Liverpool Main the pipe would be found already tapped. Could the District Council help in the matter ? It would save money eventually for it seemed a certainty that sooner or later they must come to that water supply. Nothing was of more urgent necessity for the welfare of the parish, the water supply of which was most objectionable. Any advice the Council could give him would be accepable, and if an interview would be of any use be would be most pleased to come over. It was decided that the Clerk write for plans of the proposed route of mains,and that upon their arrival the matter be taken in hand by the Parliamentary Committee. The Clerk was also instructed to ask for the insertion of the junction, but the Council were not prepared to give any guarantee.

"TEE DOCTOR AND THE COW."

Dr. Adams, medical officer, reported that a cow on the farm of Mrs. Gleave, of Manley, had been found to be afflicted with tubercle in the udier. Dr. Nivea, medical officer to the Manchester Corporation, and Dr. Vacher, the County Medical Officer, had been in communication with him en the subject, and in accordance with their suggestions the cow had beendried for the purpose of being sold: . _

Admiral loines-Parry -asked if the animal was slaugh- tered would the carcast, be fit for food ? Dr. Adams replied that under certain conditions a cow affected with tubercle would, be fit for food, but ths milk from such a cow was distinctly unfit for food. The Inspector said they had no power to dictate to Mrs. Gteave what she should do with the cow.

Mr. Woodward agreed that the Council had no power whatever ta control the sale of the cow, and Dr Adams and Dr Nivea had no right to be on the premise; when the cow was sold.

Dr. Adams said the matter had been brought to Lis notice as the district medical officer.

Mr, Pilling: We had better leave it to the doctor. He will look after the Cow. (Laughter.) Mr. Woodward : If Mrs. Gleave ceases to sell the milk from this cow what can the Manchester Authority do with it ? If she dos not sell the milk your own Dairies and Cowsheds Regulations don't apply at all. The matter then dropped.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[BY A CORRHSPONDENT.]

The speculation upon the date of the General Election, naturally, in many divisions throughout the kingdom, will cause considerable shaking of the "dry bones." Even the Northwich Division will (indeed, it has already begun to feel) some effect of the expected stir ; but it is pleasing to Liherals to know that at least they are prepared, and come the election when it will, the work of registration will have been found to be carried out so efficiently that little fears as to the result can be entertained. As remarked some weeks ago the Tories are on the out- look for an opponent against Sir John T. Brunner, Bart., M.P. As yet, however, no name has been mentioned, and should the election take place in October next it is as likely as not the Northwich Division will be uncontested.

The question of greatest importance before the Runcorn Board of Guardians on Tuesday last, was an application from their House Surgeon for a re- consideration of his salary and terms of contract and after some discussion the matter was referred to the House Committee whose recommendation will come up for consideration at the next Board meeting. From the tone of many of the members' re- marks the Do?,tor is not likely to receive much more if any more advantageous terms than those already contracted for. Besides, of late, increases of salary have been very common—and very generously met by the Guardians or District Council—and any further increase of expenlitare in that direction will begin to awaken the public. Another item of considerable importance is the fact which was illus- trated so remarkably by Mr. J. Meadowcroft, the other week, that the number of inmates appeared to be 30 less for the last week in July,l9oo,than for the corresponding week of the previous year. Naturally, with such a decrease, the percentage of sickness will be less, and the doctor may be said to have chosen an unfitting occasion upon which to prefer his request. As Admiral Jones Parry put it "why could not the House Committee reconsider the whole question of medical officer's salaries with a view to effecting a saving ?"

Runcorn Licensing Sessions are fixed for Monday the 27th inst., and it is stated that Mr. George Par- tington, of Balfour-street, intends to again apply, for an off-license for his premises at that place Already the application has more than once been refused, and notwithstanding the petitions which may be raised and signatures obtained of people who have a difficulty in obtaining inadequate drink supply, every level headed person knows that already there exist too many public-houses in Runcorn, and the duty of the Magisterial Bench lies in rather reducing than increasing licenses, They have done High-street a good turn in closing one licensed house, and a further opportunity may be presented should they feel disposed to take it.

Who said the constable had not a heart? On Thursday morning Lizzie Whelan was committed to prison for six weeks inasmuch as she had thought proper to batter the had of "her man" with an iron bar, an operation which in the opinion of the Run- corn magisterial bench was an offence against the laws and constitution of this kingdom. Lizzie having descended from the dock and her descrip- tion and record having been duly inscribeu,the duty of escorting her to her Majesty's Mansion at Knuts- ford devolved upon the father of the Runcorn police force. Naturally his course lay through Hoyle-street and at the junction of that thoroughfare with Oran. ville-street where the prisonet resided was the prisoner's little boy. The youngster began to cry piteously and the compassionate constable allowed mother and child to blenl their tears and give each other a final embrace. Truly the Runcorn police force from the Superintendent to the rawest recruit is actuated by a chivalrous spirit, and holds out a pattern which other communities of guardians of the peace would do well to copy,

The South Lancashire Tramways.

From a recently published map of the route of the proposed South Lancashire Electric Tramways it is apparent several towns will benefit very con- siderably by the new undertaking. One branch of the line will run to Westhoughton and Hindle y,an I from thence via Ashton-in-Makerfield to Haydock, St. Helens, and Liverpool ; another travels from Bolton via Atherton,Leigh,and Lowton to Earlestown and thence to St. Helens, and a third runs to Little Hulton, Walkden, Swinton, Worsley,and Patricroft. In short the large and populous industrial area, covering about 70 square miles, and which is bounded by Wigan, Bolton, Manchester Leigh, Earlestown, and St. Helens, will be intersected in all directions by the new lines. Exclusive of the larger towns, such as Manchester, Bolton, Bt. Helens, Wigan, and Leigh, the area to be served will embrace a population of half a million, The tramway will be of 4ft Bin gauge, and the cars, which will be worked by the overhead trolley sys- tem, will be handsome and commodious, and be furnished with outside seats. There will be stations at every quarter of a mile, but no doubt more fre- quent access will be arranged when toe system is in operation. Between 5 and 8 a.m. and at mid-day on Saturday the fare will be -Id per mile, while id per mile will be the maximum charge at any time. A great convenience to the public will also accrue from the system by the carriage of parcels, etc., on the cars. As the Tramways Bill has received. the Royal Assent it is anticipated the promoters will not delay in utilising their powers.

WARRINGTON. Coming Events.

--- I We would remind our readers tnat next Wednes- day and Thursday (the 22ad and 23rd met) Shrews- bury Great Floral Me which has been described as the largest and grandest fl iwer show ever organized in either London or the Provinces, will be held at Shrewsbury, when over £l,OOO will be distributed in prizes. There will be many attractions in addition to the show. Each day the combined bands of the Royal 'Rue Guards (Blue), the Coldstream Guards, the Portsmouth Royal Marines, and the Scots Guards Pipers, to the number of 105 per- formers, will contribute selections of music, and some of the most accomplished acrobats, &c., will also appear and twice daily (at four and seven o'clock) there will be balloon ascents to the height of a thousand feet by the Crystal Palace Aeronaut, Further, there will be a horse-leaping competition, for which £lOO will be given in prizes. In Cadishead, irlain, Astley, and on Chat and Car- rington Mosses, belonging to the Manchester Cor- poration, excellent crops of oats, wheat and rye are being obtained. The ears are lull, and the straw short. Good yields ot tuberous-rooted produce are likewise assured. A feature of the harvest Is the dearth of Irish labourers, a large number of those employed being fustian cutters and other, whose trades are in a depressed condition. Music in the Park.

A factory operative named 'ehomas Marsh, of 51, Halme-street, Manchester-road, committed suicide by hanging himself on Saturday morning. He had suffered from influenz I for some time and was ins very depressed state of mind. About eleven o'clock on Saturday morning his wife left the house, and en her return aoout noon she missed him. She searched the house, and found him hanging from a rope suspended from a bedpost. She at once cut the rope. The police were summoned, but life was found to be extinct. At the inquest on Monday evidence was given by the widow and P.C. Newsy. —A juror said he had known deceased for a great many years. He was a very inoffensive man. Since his illness he had been very much depressed.—A verdict to the effect that deceased committed suicide whilst of unsound mind was returned. Warrington Engineers at Welbeck Abbey. The annual picnic of Messrs Rylands Bros.' engineering staff took place on Saturday, the venue selected being the well-known seat of the Duke of Portland at Welbeck Abbey. The party numbered 17, and a special saloon -being placed at their dis- posal, the journey to Worksop was thoroughly en- joyed. Arriving hare, carriages conveyed the party to the Lion Hotel, and lunch having been heartily enjoyed char-a-banes were chartered, and the drive through Sherwood Forest proved thoroughly interesting, the outing being made all the more enjoyable by the graphic descriptions given by a guide. Arriving back at their headquarters, a capi- tal dinner was served, and needless to add, full jus- tice was done. There was the usual convivial hour at the close, and on arriving at Warrington there was a cencensus of opinion that the visit to the Dukeries was one of the most enjoyeble yet ex peri.nced. The arrangements were ably carried out by Messrs R Savory and J Jeffery. Independent Order of Good Templars. Warrington Field Club.

On Saturday the members of the Field Club travelled to West Kirby for the purpose of visiting Hilbre Island. The weather was beautiful and the tide favourable, which means that the members were able to be at the north-western corner of the island about five o'clock, at low water of a spring tide. This affords the best opportunity of examin- ing a considerable area of rock which is as crowded with living creatures as Sankey-street ,tt nine o'clock on a Sunday evening. Collectors were busy securing exquisitely beautiful seaweeds, hydroids which to an inexperienced eye look more like plants than animals, anemones, alcvonaria, with various crustaceans and molluscs. The most interesting of the molluscan dwellers on Hilbre is the pholas, a sort of mussel which bores holes in the rock, often half a yard deep, and which manifests its disappro- val of scientific research by indignantly squirting a jet of water at the meddlesome naturalist who endeavours to capture it. Despite their protests these venerable conservatives were dragged front their lairs and ruthlessly butchered to make a Field Club holiday. Tired, wet and hungry, but happy and triumphant, the membPrs returned at sundown.

Local Oddfellowship—Presentatio-n—:

On Saturday, at the monthly meeting of the Garibaldi Lodge of the National Independent Order of Oddfellows (Warrington District) an interesting ceremony took place. He hoped he would continue to give his services for the bene- fit of Oddfellowship. Bre Chidlaw, in responding. said it was his ambition when first he became an Oddfellow to gain such an honour, and he had to thank the officers of the lodge for the help and ad- vice which had been given to him time after time. He especially thanked the lodge for their handsome itt The remainder ot the evening was pleasantly spent, songs being contributed by Bros Burgess, Emson, Blackshaw, and J Hughes. Brass Band Contest at Eastham.—Warrington

Borough Band Disqualified. The second annual band contest took place in Eastham Gardens on Saturday afternoon. The weather was all that could be desired,and the beau- tiful grounds were patronised by a very large number of visitors, many of whom were relatives or supporters of bandsmen taking part in the con- test, and had travelled long distances in order to be present at the performance. Some sixteen bands entered for the contest,and all put in an appearance but one, the Warrington Bosough Band, was die- qualified en account of some irregularity. The test piece was" Songs of England," published by Messrs Wright and Round, of Liverpool. Mr J 0 Shepherd was the judge, and in giving his award he expressed his disappointment with the performances generally though he had, he said, beard some very good solo playing. At the Warrington Police Court en Monday, Julia Craghan (16), a fustian cutter, residing at 13, Dutton- street, was charged with stealing from the person of Nellie Mather, whilst in Bank-street at two o'clock on Saturday afternoon, the sum of 4s 61, the money of Ellen Buchanan.—The Chief Constable said that for some I time p lc, the police had been receiving reports of children being robbed in the streets in a very ingenious way by a girl or girls a little older than themselves. Officers had been specially detailed to enquire into and if possible, detect this pernicious form of crime. On Saturday the little girl Mather, who was eleven years of age was sent by her grandmother to obtain payment for some work that the grandmother bad done. As she was returning she met the prisoner, who was a perfect stranger to her, in Buttermarket-street. Prisoner said to her" Your mother wants you to go for some flour" and thr girl said " Whore is my mother ?" Prisoner re- plied that she was in Bank-street and offered to take the chid to her. Whilst they were on the way prisoner said "Let me look at your money" and the girl innocently let her take the money which amounted to 4s. 11d. Prisoner then said" Your mother says you have to get some flour" and gave back to her what purported to be the money wrapped up in some paper, and disappeared. The girl, on opening the paper found it contained only sixpence in copper- In- formation was given to the police and inquiries were set on foot and prisoner was apprehended by P.C. Gough. She was brought to the Police Station and stoutly deni€ d all knowledge of the theft and endeavoured to prove an alabi in a very cute and clever way for a girl of her age. She impressed the officer so much with her statement that she was allowed to go, pending inquiries. Further inquiries were made and later in the afternoon prisoner was rearrested and brought to the Police Ratio,' and admitted having stolen the money. She said she had given her mother 2s 6d of the money and had hidden is 6d behind a gate in Church- street. An officer accompanied her to the hiding place and found her statement to be correct. Prisoner's mother stated that the girl gave her Ss as if the money was her wagee. She had been neglecting her work dur- ing the week and it was suggested, made up her wages with the stolen 21 61 in order to blindfold her mother as to her doings during the wee.—The Bench committed the prisoner to gaol for a month in number two division. —Ann Peers, aged twenty years,ls, Longford-street,was also charged with stealing from the person of Mary M'Cue, a little girl of 6, residing in Kerfoot-street, at 11.30 on Thursday last, the sum of 31. The prosecutrix was sent with 31. to buy some meat, when she was stopped by the prisoner, who asked her where she was going. She offered to get the meat for her and the child handed her the money. Prisoner then made off. When brought to the police star.ion she at first denied the theft, but subsequently admitted it.—She was sent to gaol for fourteen days in the second division A BRUTAL FATHER SMARTLY DEALT WITH.

William ad Clara Bibby, of 12, Warburton-street, were summoned at the Borough Police Court on Tues- day before Alderman Sutton (in the chair) and Mr. Birchall, for neglecting and abandoning their three children, Richard Stanley, William, and James. The male defendant pleaded guilty and the female defendant net guilty.—Mr. Browne, who appeared for the N.S.P. C.C. Society, said the male defendant was a forge labourer and earned 194 per week. He also sold ice cream and made profit on that. Detective Delahay had reason to go to the house on the 7th of July to serve a sumnaens on the man for breaking a window at the house of his father-in-law. He foetid the house empty and in a terrible condition. The floor did not appear to have been washed for months. There was very little furniture—two broken chairs and an old table. In the back place there was no furniture at all. Upstairs in the front room there was an old bed. stead covered with a dirty sack. In the back place was something similar. The detective made inquiries and foune! that the children were wandering about the street.—Mr. Birchall : Do you say that he made ice cream in tele place P—Mr. Browne: No sir, he bought it outside I hope. (Laughter.) Cmetinuing, he said the child res were afterwards taken in by the grandmother and subsequently were removed to the Workhouse. The wile made allegations against the husband, and the hus- band's friends made al'egations against the wife. He (Mr. Browne) and the Inspector tioeught that trouble had been caused by the interference oh the mother-in- law. The wife left the house because the man had stood over her with a knife, and the neighbours bad had to get.her through the window to prevent.as they said,his murdering her. The cuildren were in a wretched filthy, and negleeted condition. That was purely the wife's fault. She had however, been suffering from a tumour in the neck, and had been an in- mate of the Infirmary fteon July 17th to 31st.— Detective Delahaye Inspector Hackett, and Mr. Garnett (relieviug officer) gave evidence in support. —lda Woods, I€l, Warhurton-street, said she had seen Bibby illuse his wife.—Ellen Potts, 14, Warberton-etreet, said she bad given the wife food.—Biboy stated that he had lost work through his wife's neglect. She stayed out two or three nights ira a week. The children always had plenty to eat. She had driven him away.—Mrs. Bibby was sworn, and said she had only had two weeks' wages trom her husband since Christmas. He had been laying out the money and drinking. At one time he was on the shop book for eleven pints of beer. She had been living on dry bread and sugar. He was a good husband, but had been ruined by a drunken mother. One day he threatened to murder her. He said ; "I will operate upon you. I will burst that (the tumour) with my fist. lam going to murder you and put you nine feet under the sod."—Mrs. Cowen gave evidence, and the charge against the wife was withdrawn.—Bibby was committed to gaol for two months with hard labour.

SEQUEL TO AN ASSAULT ON A CONSTA BLE.-TIIE RESULT

OF GIVING EVIDENCE FOR THE POLICE.

On Thursday at the Warrington Borough Court,before Alderman Fairclough (chairman) and ether magistrates, William Cunningham was charged on a warrant with making use of threats towards Kate Taylor on the 14th inst.—The Chief Constable explained that on Wednesday the complainant made application to the Bench for a warrant against the prisoner under the following cir- cumstances. About a week ago the complainant came forward voluntarily and gave evidence on behalf of the police in the prosecution of a young man who violently assaulted a police officcr who had since b€ en off duty and was complimented by the Mayor for his courage and pertinacity in following up the two prisoners. It was mainly upon the woman's evidence that the man was convicted and since then she had been subjected to continual annoyance by people who lived in the neighbourhood and had gone about in fear. Her principal persecutor had been the prisoner, and on Wedne3day the complainant cam,' before the Bench and stated that she was afraid to go about on account of the man's threats. The magistrates on his (Mr. Talbot's) recommendation kindly granted a free warrant. It was an intolerable thing that a person who came for- ward to give evidence should be intimated by ruffians of this class.—Complainant, who resides at 8, James'- street stated that on Monday night the prisoner who resides in Bowcock-street was " carrying on " about her and on the following morning she was passing along the street whea she met him. He said she had given a fellow three months and that if she came down there he would kick her from the top of the street to the bottom. She was afraid of the man because she knew he was quite capable of carrying out his threat:—Prisoner alleged that com- plainant was drunk and said he did not remember using the threat.—The Chief Constable said that was his 19th appearance.—Prisoner was bound over to keep the peace for six months in one surety of £5, and ordered to pay the costs (153 6d) or in default a month's imprisonment.

They had been in and out of the Workhouse for the past eight or nine years, and during that time the prisoner had lived either in the Workhouse or In gaol. The family had cost the Guardians some hundred pounds, and this year alone had cost £3O or £4O. Warrington County Petty Sessions

WEDNESDAY.—Before Captain Withington (in the ohair),and Messrs. J. Ball and J. Tinsley.

ALLEGED ASSAULT BY AN EMPLOYER.—Edgar Jowast, contractor, Winwick, was summonel for assaulting Martin Burke on August Ist at Winwlck. arid complainant also claimed from him 6s for work done. Mr Browne appeared for the defendant and pleaded not guilty—Martin Burke, labourer, said he was employed by the defendant to do some work in the laying of a water main at Winwick on July 30th, and he finished work on July 3tst, having worked the whole of the afternoon of July 30th, and the morning of July 31st. He then asked the defendant for his money 6s, whereuoon defendant pulled off his coat and thrashed him.—Miehael Kane. a labourer lately employed by the defendant corrobor- ated.—Mr Browne for the defence said that the defendant was a contractor for the Warrington Corporation Water Works Committee, and had in his employ about 25 men, On Monday morning. July 30th, Burke asked him for a job, and the defendant gave him work and atit.4 him to atart at once. He, however, only started in the af.ernoon. On Tuesday he worked five hours, and in the afternoon went away to a public-house, where he remained the whole after- noon. Then, under the influence of drink, he demanded his wages and the defendant plainly toll him that he would have them on the following Sitturcity,adcling that he had no right:to leave his work. Burke threatened the defendant, and would not leave the place, but tried to gat the other men to leave work. Defendant asked him three times to leave the place. and on refusing he turned him away, and that w .5 the assault complained of. The wages due to Burke were 3s 9d, not 6s. and the defendant was not legally bound to pay him, but to claim from him in the County Court for damages, because be did not work a fulr week. He was however, willing to pay Burke 3s 9d. —Burke accepted 3s 9d and withdrew the charge of assault. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. _ Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

Theusual service in connection with the above-named Society was fairly well attended on Sunday last. Mr. Francis Brown presided, and delivered a brief address, his subject being " Naaman the leper." Mr. W. Williams, of Manchester, was the speaker,whose address was highly appreciated. He based his remarks on what he considered to be the most pressing need of the present day, "Practical Christianity." Mr. Williams also sang a solo " Thus saith the Lord " (Handel), which was well received. The Rev. W. T. Ball, of Brightm, and Mr. B. Farrar also took part in the service.

RUNCORN FOOTBALL CLUB.

On Friday night the committee of the Runcern Foot- ball Club entertained the players to a supper at the headquarters, Wilson's Hotel, and the function was made the medium of a chat over the position of the club and the prospects for next seasoa. Tue whole of the players have not yet signed oa, but are expected to do so at an early date and practice games c ;mmenced, At the close ofilast sea3on Houghton, tns brilliant full-back seemed inclined to rest on his laurels and retire from the game. The general meeting, however, re-elected him captain by e large majority, and he has now announced his intention to again don the myrtle-green jersey and endeavour to lead the team to emulate the victories of the past. In the Northern Union Cup ties the club has been better drawn than ever before. Should the team have the good fortune to survive, the three first rounds are to b e played at tihome. On last ar's form Wigan ought to fall victima in the first round, and the probable opponents in the second round are Leigh, a match which would furnish a stern struggle with th probablities in favour of Runcorn. Halifax are likely to be the opposine side in the third round,an as the match would be on Runcore soil, the home team ought to win. Considerable alterations are being mide to the ground. The playing Ijtch has been removed about twenty yards hig,her up the field, in order to steer clear of the low lying land on the stand side which in wet weather is often a qulgmire. Toe stand, too, has beeß pulled down. It was tar from up-to-date, and did not provide the accommodation requisite. In its place is being erected a stand of better design, capatle of holding about 800 spectators with comfort, and with more convenient arrangements for the Press, who under the conditions that have prevailed hitherto, were often working in the dark ere the end of a match wae reached, and too often had their view of the game obstructed. These alterations will absorb the greater portion of the balance the committee have at the bank. Toe com- mittee, who behaved very generously to the players throughout, last season, propose to give the players a day at the seaside ere the season opens.

WARRINGTON RURAL DISTRICT

COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural District Council was held at Bewsey Chamb..rs on Wednesday afternoon, Mr. Joshua Ball presiding. There were also present :—Messrs J W Knowles,J Dignum,W Houghton, Brooks, T Halton. H Thornton, P Longton, R Forshaw, J C Sutton (clerk), 8 Sutton (assistant clerk), F W Kerfoot (inspector), W B Newcombe (surveyor), Wright (assistant surveyor).

MEDICAL OFFICER'S' REPORT —CASES OF SCARLET FEVER. Dr. SEPHTON wroze from Llandudno saying that he was sorry that he should not be able to attend the meet- ing of the Council, as he should remain at Llandudno uutil Friday. During the month several cases of scarlet fever and diphtheria had been reported from Burton- wood, Three cases of scarlatina had been sent in hospital,and one case of diphtheria proved fatal. Several cases of scarlatina occurred at Hollins Green, and one terminated fatally. Dr. Seegeaut reported that a com- plaint had been made to him about the deposit of manure at Fiddler's Ferry. He referred the matter to the Inspector, who would report thereon.

BRC-010 said he was sorry that Dr. Sephton was not present as there were rumours as regarded the scarlet fever at Hollins Green, that patients were going about peeling. That was the worst thing possible to cause contagion.

It was dee-iJed that Mr. Kerfoot should communicate with Dr. Sephton about the matter aai report at the next meeting.

THE ALLEGED NUISANCE AT FIDDLER'S FERRY.

With r ,fereuce to the depo,,,it ot maaure at Fiddler's Ferry, Mr Kerfoot stated that it was the same old com- plaint. The station master had complained that it took four days to di,charge a boat load ot manure. At present MPEI were very scarce and could not be obtained to dis- charge the manure in a shorter space of time. Mr. Kerfoot read the letter he had sent to the County Medi- cal Officer stating that on Saturday the 21st July the station master Unformed him that another boat load had been deposited and he (Mr. Kerfoot) gave instructions to Mr Warburton, toe owner, to remove the manure. Ho visited the place again on the following Monday and found that Warburton's mea were engaged in carting the, mreure away and it would probably be removed that week.

BURTONWOOD WATER SUPPLY.

Messrs William and Joseph Houghton, the Chapel House, Burtonwood, wrote aa follows :—" We as pro- perty owners wish the water scheme to be carried through Buttonwood. We own several cottages neA. the Board School and have no means of getting water. 'Therefore we think it very desirable that water should be brought through the village." The following letter was read from Mr George Anders:—"l am part owner of nine cottages and a shop at Buttonwood and am put to great inconvenience for a water supply, having t • carry it from a tank belonging to the Collins Green Colliery Company, a m,tter of a quarter of a mile. If your Council Can see their way to carry the Collins Green water supply to Burton wood village, I am sure all pro- perty owners would he benefited and that it would be for the well being of the parish." Mr. FousuAw: I thiuk all the Council can do is to refer the matter to the Town Clerk.

The CLERK said it was for the Council to say whether they could carry out the extension. The Town Clerk would go to the extent they wiqhed him so far as he could go. The Parish Council of Burtonwood had better consider the position as to how far they could advise the Council to act upon the letters. Mr. FORSHAW said ho was very anxious to see the water supply extended to School Brow but still he wanted it to he done in a bisines,-liko manner,so as not to put a burden on the ratepayers. It was decith-d that the Council should obtain the opinion of the Parish Council in the matter and that Mr. Forshaw should bring the subject undur the notice of the last named body.

NluspaaTr, the clerk to the Board, said that the desire of the Board was that the Rural Distric,-; Council should take the swage from the new asylum either into their own sewers, or by coupling up with the sewers of the Warrington Corporation. A BRILLIANT RUNCORN MEDICO.

The Microbe of Elephantiasis.

The exceedingly important discovery of the parasite of elephantiasis in the mosquito by the members of the expedition of the Liverpool School of Tropical Medicine now in Nigeria, recalls the fact that the gentleman in charge of that expedition is one of Runcorn's most brilliant young medicos. This is Dr H. A. Annett, who is not yet thirty years of age. His career has been one, record of successes. Born in Nottingham, in June, 1871, he was a young boy when brought to Runcorn by his father, Mr T. EL Annett, schoolmaster of Trinitya Day Schools; and Dr Annett is re- garded as a native of the town, and the towns- people are greatly proud of hie achievement. A brief narration of his history cannot fail to be inter- esting. After receiving the rudiments of education. under the tutelage of his father, young Annett became a student at Liverpool College, Seaw-street. In 1889 he commenced his medical career at University Colleges Liverpool,having gained the Roger Lyon-Jones entrance scholarship, awarded for two years. Duricie 1891-2 he enjoyed the distinction of winning both the junior and senior gold medals for the subjEcts of the respective years, and else secured the senior Roger Lyon-Jones scholarship,tenable for two years. The following year he won the medal for pathology, and in 1894 he obtained his degree of Bachelor of Medicine and Surgery of Victoria University, with honours. Dr.Annett determined' to devote his knowledge and abilities ,to the important subject of public health,and in 1895 his studies in public health and bacteriology earned him the diploma of pub- lic health granted by Victoria University. Dr. Annett's 'field of operations was greatly widened in 1896. BesideS his training in medicine and in sanitation, Dr. Annett had also qualified himself for 3 university degree of science, and was thus eligible for one of the 1851 exhi- bition scholarships. Her Majesty's Commissioaers in the year named granted Dr. Annett one of these scholar- ships for two years, in order to " study and investigate the relations of the diseases of animals to public health., With characteristic energy and determination, Dr" Annett commenced the task of solving the problem set before him. For six months he studied the infectious diseases of animals at the Royal Veterinary College. London, under Professor M'Fadyean,and than proceeded to Berlin, and there enrolled himself a student at the Royal Veterinary College, and at Professor Koch's Insti- tute for Hygiene and Infectious Disease. Here hygienic problems—chemical, bacteriological, and sanitary—were wrested with, under the guidance of Professor Koch,pro , bably the greatest hygienic scientist of the world. Dr. Annett entered into the field of original research ia regard to ranschbrand (black quarter of cattle), and malignant celeina and braxy of sheep, and in endeavouriog to produce measures which would pre- vent or minimise the ravages of these scourges. Other diseases in animals, including tuberculosis, swine fever, and anthrax were investigated, and German methods of sanitary administratien,especially in regard to milk and meat supplies, also absorbed a portion of the energetic young medical student's time. Returning to England' Dr. Annett, in 1899, was appointed administrator irg, tropical pathology and bacteriology at the Liverpoel 'sschool of Tropical Medicine, where he continued aluanle work of research. A still further scholastic honour was in store for Dr. Annett. At midsummer. 1899, he was made a doctor of medicine at Victoria College, and he was given the highest of university awards, the geid medal, for his dissertad° upon the subject of his work in Germany. In tbe autumn he accompanied Major Ross in his malarial a pedition to West Africa, anu the success which attended that expedition is still fresh in the public mind. In the spring of the present year,the Tropical School determined upon another expedition with the same and in view,and Dr Annett had the honour and responsibility conferred upon him of being placed in command, with the result that a formerly unknown bacillus has been discovered. Dr. Annett has made numerous contributions to medic& literature, displaying great originality and research.

Lova! Order of Ancient Shepherds, All. The annual meeting in connection with the War- rington Juvenile Burial Society and Widow and Orphans' Fund belonging to the above order took place at the British Workman's public-house Horse- market-street on Monday. There was a very fair attendance of members during the evening who seemed to manifest great interest in the whole of the proceedings. Tee meeting was presided over by the President PPCS Frederick Massey, and supported by the Vice-President PC S Thomas Miller, the treasurer PPC S James Parr, and trus- tees P M William Holmes, PPCS Peter Edgar, PPCS William Eckersley. the general secretary Lawrence Weir. The Chairman in opening the proceedings asked to be allowed to extend to all a hearty welcome on the occasion of the 29th annual meeting in connection with the Juvenile Burial Society and the 35th belonging to the Widow and Orphans' Fund. He then referred to the work done during the past year and complimented the mem- bers upon the very substantial gain that had been made during the year's transactions which had ex- ceeded the previous year's gain by some pounds and these gains would continually take place so long as they kept the present machinery they now had. He felt certain all the officers had the interest of the societies at heart. The general secre- tary, in presenting his annual report, showed that the total worth of the Juvenile Society amounted to £2,140 28 8d as compared with .£1,967 is 3d of the previous year, making a gain for the year of £173 is 5d and a total membership of 1,280. The report further showed that the Widow and Orphans Fund amounted to £199 2s 6d as against £204 17s 4d in the previous year, and figures furnished by the secretary for the information of the members of the Juveni:e Society, shewed they had made a steady gain during the past seven years, averaging £l5/ 108 st-el, or a total gain for that period of £1,060 13s 3cl. (Applause.) The auditors P.P.C.•_,. Peter Edgar. and P,M. John Jones in giving in their re- port stated they had examined the various accounts and registers kept by the secretary and also seen and examined the treasurer's and collectors books They had great pleasure in certifying that the same were correct and satisfactory. The bank books, Corporation bonds, mortgage deeds, and vouchers which had been submitted to them for inspection were in perfect order. The manner in which the general secretary and the treasurer performed their respective duties reflected very highly upon those officials, and they could from past experience, assure the members that the societies had in them two excellent officers. The election of officers and committee next took place with the following result :—Bro Th0223a9 Miller, P.C.S., president; 13r1) John Haley, D.P US., vice president; general secretary,P.P.C.S. Lawrence Weir; treasurer, P.P.C.B. James Parr ; trustees, P.M. William Holmes, P.P.C.S, Peter Edgar,P.P.C.S. William Eckersley ; auditor, Bro John Jones, p.m.; committee, Bros John Jones, Joseph Whitlow, john w Sykes, Henry Cook, Samuel Bradshaw, Willian2 Ball, Joseph Adams, George Howard, David Harris, Henry Warburton,James Gresty. The remainder of the business was completed which consisted of several very important matters bearing upon the general welfare of the members. The following members took part in the discussion : Bros William Sckersley, Peter Edgar, John Jones, Peter Kirkham, James Lowe, Samuel Blythe and others, A vote 01 thanks was accorded to the retiring President, P.P.C.S. Frederick .Slassey, for the able and efficient manner he had conducted the business during the past year. Bro Massey, in response, said the kin', teeling displayed towards him that evening ha u rewarded him for his labours, and he assured them he should always be ready to render any assistanc! that lay in his power towards furthering OE society's progress. TO SUPPORT HE INFIRMARY.

The Forthcoming Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival.

The arrangements for the fancy dress parade and carnival which is to be held on Saturday the 25th just, to augment the funds of the Infirmary,are now in full swing. Strenuous efforts are being made in order that the event shall prove a success, and it is hoped to realize not less than £2OO. The procession will leave the Town Hall at 5.30 pin and proceed along Sankey-street, Rutter- market-street, Irlam-street, Church-street to the lamp opposite the Parish Church, and then along Mersey-street, Bridge-Areet, Horsemarket- street, Winwick-street, Tanners-lane, Froghall-lane, Priestly-street, Powys-street, Liverpool-road, Bank Quay Bridge, Parker-street, Wilson Patten-street, Bold-street, Palmya Square to the Town Hall. The following organizations have promised to take part in the parade :-Ist Volunteer Battalion South Lancashire Regiment Cycle Corps, Warrington Cycling Club, Warrinzton Royal Cycling Club, War- rington Thursday Cycling Club, Padgate Cycling Club, ‘Vidnas Cycling Club, Widnes Wheelers Cycling Club, Warrington Cricket Club, Warrington Rugby Football Club, Warrington Association Football Club, Warrington Harriers, Sankey Harriers, members of the Gymnasium, St. John Ambulance Brigade, Warrington Fire Brigade, local Friendly Societies, and most of the local brass bands. All cyclists in fancy dress are invited, and valuable prizes will be offered for the best " get- ups." At a meet'ng of the Committee on Thursday evening, Mr H Ashton was appointed chief marshal. The Mayoress called a meeting of young ladies willing to collect which was held on Monday. It is proposed to have 500 collectors and the public generally are requested to assist in maintaining order, and particularly in protecting those ladies who are collecting. The demonstration will un- doubtedly exceed anything of the kind previously attempted in the town and it is to be hoped the funds of the Infirmary will be materially benefited by it.
