THE RECENT RAINFALL :IN WARRINGTON.

Interesting Figures.

We are indebted to the Borough Surveyor (Mr. T Longdin) for the following interesting figures re- lating to the recent heavy rainfall in IVarrington. It appears that from 9 a.m. on August 2nd to 9 a.m. on the following day, 489,055 tons or equal to 109,537,120 gallons of water fell on the area of the Borough ; and that between 3.25 p.m. and 410 p.m., or within 43 minutes on the 3rd of August, 343,330 tons or equal to 73,149,130 gallons fell. This is a phenomenal fall of rain in the Borough. It is calculated by the best authorities that 75 per cent, of this water passes away through the sewers, The rainfall this month up to the Btn inst. has been 4'16 whilst in the corresponding period of last year it was only .12.

WEEKLY REPORT REPORT OF THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

Week Ending Saturday, August 4th, 1900. Durim: the week the following births and deaths were registered : _ .

Births. Deaths. .Warrington Registration_ District 51 20

The excess of births over deaths was 34

The Birth Rate was equal ta 43.2 per I,oog. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 16.0 per 1,000, against 160 the average of the previous four weeks, and against 171 the corresponding period of last year. The death- rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 4 8 per 1,000, against 2.4 the average rate of the previous four weeks, and against 72 the corresponding period of 1899. The ages of mortality were- -0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65 up. Total. 7 5 1 6 1 20

The deaths included :-2 from whoopi-g cough. 4 from diarrhcea, 3 from phthisis, 3 from lung diseases, 1 from heart disease, 1 from influenza, 6 from all other causes.

The:Vollowing case of infectious disease has been reported during the week :—Scarlet fever, one case. Total number of patients in hospital week ending Saturday,?August 4th. 8.

_ METEOROLOGY—The mean temperature was 60.5 Fah., and therefore 55 Deg. lower than the preceding week. The maximum temperature 76F., was recorded on the 30th July and the minimum temperature 50 F.. on the 30th July The Rainfall measured 3'75 inch. The general direc- tion was S.W.

JOHN H. GOIINALL, Medical Officer of Health.

Presentation at Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Sunday School.

There was an interesting gathering at Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Sunday School after the closing of the school on Sunday afternoon, when the teachers and elder scholors remained behind to make a presentation to Mr. Prank Light on his re- tirement from the duties of secretary in conse- quence of his removal to Wakefield. The presenta- tion consisted of a handsome Gladstone bag bearing the inPcription "Presented to Mr. Frank Light by the Sankey Bridges Wesleyan Sunday school for his faithful services as secretary. August 12, 1900." Mr. W Brough and Mr. S. Blythe, the superintend- ents of the school, made the presentation, and spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr. Light's services and of his devotion to the work. It was with deep regret that they parted with him but they were glad to know that his prospects in life were brightening, and in his new duties at Wake- field they wished him :very possible success. Short speeches were also made by Mr. R flighley, Mr R Sudlew, Mr J Corrodine and Mr P Taylor.—ln ac- cepting the gift, Mr. Light thanked the subscribers for their present and also the speakers for the kind and:encouraging words they had given utterance to. He was exceedingly sorry that circumstances had rendered it necessary for him to sever his connec- tion with the school. It had been a pleasure to him to take a share in the work and he would carry through life a pleasant recollection of the hours spent in S.aukey Bridges Wesleyan Sunday b'chool.

Letter to the Obitcr.

The Handy Man. 1 Dear sir,—Eagland feels that she owes a deep debt of gratitude to her Seamen and Royal Marines for their gallant deeds in South Africi, and now again in China. Whenever there is a scrimmage, ashore or aft )at, Jac and Joe are in for it, and never come out second best. Vast sums of money have been raised for wives, orphans, and mothers, and although great energy is expended in the administration of these funds, there must still be missing links. Your paper is widely read, and the eye of some Rervice man may light upon this letter. He knows fu'l wel that my heart is ever with our men of the Navy and those dear to them, and I only want to know of a case of distress unrelieved to get that case helped. I ask my service friends and others to let me know of any unrelieved and deserving oases, and I will do my best for them. While much is done for widows and mothers, but little is done for Jack and Joe. Tke Naval Brigade are the fortunate fellows, but every day men are invalided out of the Service— without work, without pension, in weak health, and often with insufficient clothing. This state of affairs has only to be known to be remedied. Help for a few weeks would set many a bluejacket or marine, worn by sickness, on his feet again. A. warm suit of clothes on invaliding would be a boon to many a man on leaving the hospital for civil life ; and small sums of money can be wisely expended in paying the railway fare of mother or wife to enable her to look once more into the face of the dying son or husband, and to hear his last words. I am ready to do all this for our men—for richly do they deserve it—in their time of weakness and sorrow.— Yours very truly,

AGNES E. WESTON.

Royal Sailors' Rest, Portsmouth.

LATEST NEWS.

CHINA. The Allied Forces Reported at Pekin,

SHANGHAI, WEDNESDAY.

The " Daily Express" says the Allies are reported to have reached Pekin on Monday. Chinese official news confirms this statement, but there are no details.

PARIS. WEDNESDAY NIGHT.

The" Agence Nationale" have received informa- tion to the effect that the international army has reached Pekin.

A Chefoo telegram, dated the 14th, published in the "New York Journal" on Thursday morning, says :—I learn from thoroughly trustworthy Chinese sources that the allies reached Pekin on Monday. I hive every reason to believe that the allied armiee forced an entrance, and that th e foreigners in Pekin were rescued to-day.

The Allied Advance Successfuli Chinese Flying. Enemy Abandoning Guns.

AUGUST 9TH, (VIA CHEFOO, AUGUST 13TH).

The enemy have retreated from their position at Ilushiwa. Our f3rward march is now practically unopposed. The Bengal Lancers and Japanese Cavalry continue to lead the advance. They are in close contact with the enemy, and pressing him hard. The Chinese are flying before our cavalry charges and have been severly punished in the pur- suit. Their losses are . great. The enemy have abandoned many guns, which have fallen into our hands. The heat is intense. Notwithstanding this we are making forced marches. We are all suffer- ing terribly.

Double, Execution at Armley Gaol At nine o'clock onThursday morning Tom Mellor, for child murder at Holbeck,and Charles Backheuse. for shooting a policemen at Swinton, were hanged at Armley Gaol. Billington was the executioner, No reporters were admitted to witness the execu- tion. The morning was gloomy, but a crowd assembled in front of the gaol to see the black flag hoisted.

THE WAR.

LATEST TELEGRAMS.

The Elands River Garrison Official Despatch.

The War Office issued the following official des- patch at eleven o'clock on Wednesday night From Lord Roberts to the Secretary of State of War. PRETOEM, TUE MAY. Colonel Helyar, who was reported missing on 31st July, was, I regret to say, murdered by the Boers. His body was found yesterday by Colonel Hickman, and is being brought here for interment. Two men believed to ba the murderers will be trio I by curt martial. The small force at Elands River Station, which i t was feared,as stated in my telegram of the Bth inst., had been captured b 7 the en emy,was holding ow; on the 10th inst., on which date Colonel awe was able to despatch a messenger, who reached Mafeking yesterday. There had been sixty-seven casualties. A lieutenant of the Queensland Mounted Infantry was the only officer killed. Carrington has been ordered to proeeed at once to Zeerust, and lan Hamilton has been informed of the necessity of affording Hon assistance as soon as pas- sible. .

Hamilton's mounted troops should be less than forty miles from Elands River Station this afternoon. He reports that Lieutenant F G Newton, Queens- land Mounted Infantry, was wounded in the nigh ! on the 12th inst. ; severe, but bone not broken. No fresh news from Methum or Kitchener, who are reported some distance from the telegraph line.

Clery reports tint twenty more burghers surren- dered to him yrsterday.

The Pursuit of De Wet.

KRUGERSDORP. THURSDAY. De Wet has crossed the railway and is making north to jiu Delarey, who holds Rustenburg. His force is already reported to be beyond Ventersdorp. Lord Kitchener, with other Generals, is pursuing, and pressing him bard. The Orange River Colony is now clear of the enemy. except for a small force under Olivier in the neighbourhood of Heilbron, General Buller and General French have joined hands. Opposing Hunter's Advance. Boers Forced to Retire. Hunter's match north was opposed south of Heilbron by the Boers, who held Spitz Kop with six guns. The British, however, forced the enemy to retire. Good Results of Buller's Advance. TWEYELAAR, WEDNESDAY. General Buller's force has reaohed here. The opposition in the south-eastern corner of the Trans- vaal has been broken by the surrender of the Staaderton commando.

"With Lord Bobs at Ladysmith."

Messrs. F J Norman and W Timmins, sat in judgment at the Court House, Runcorn, on Tuesday evening upon one James McDonald, a one-legged pedlar of unfixed residence. The charge against the prisoner was that of going about endeavouring to procure charitable dona- tions by certain fradulent proceeding. Sergeant Samuel Gregory testified to the prisoner calling at his house 14, Lightburn-street, and tolling a story about being in- valided home from South Africa where he had been serving "with Lord Roberts, at Ladysmith." Witness believed the story and parted with 2i. Gregory was wise enough, however, to enquire whPrs the self styled hero was putting up and was told at Mrs Kelly's, Fryer- at. Now as is well-known Sergeant Gregory knows every- body in Fryer-street, and the name sounding unfamiliar to him he hastened to asce:tain whether Mrs Kelly did reside in that locality. He found it was not so, and pursuing his enquiries further learnt that prisoner had been to Sergeant Instructor Callaghan and told a similar story. Sergeant Callaghan asked for the man's discharge, but it was not forthcoming. Still the generous instruc- tor parted with certain coppers out of wunpassion.— Prisoner admitted to the Bench that he had been going about telling a lot of lies, but that he had only done so as a kind of joke, inasmuch, as there was so much war knocking about that it was the only thing that would take at the present time and he did it to excite sym- nattiv.—lt further transpired that the man had lost his leg in a reaping machine.—The Chairman described the offence as a despicable one, for the prisoner's conduct was likely to prevent those really deserving men who came home incapacitated from the war, from receiving anything in the form of charity. He would be sent to prison for 14 days.

MILITARY NOTES.

Captain Staveley, RA., who letely gave up the post of Staff Captain for Royal Artillery in this district to go to South Africa has been promoted Major in the Royal Field Artillery. Second Lieutenant Johnston just appointed to the South Lancashire Regiment on getting his com- mission from the Royal Military College, Sandhurst, joins the details of the Ist Battalion of 40 regi- ment.

In consequence of Captain Waters of the 2nd Battalion South Lancashire Regiment being placed on half-pay owing to ill health there is now a vacancy for a Captain in the regiment. Lieutenant Woods is the senior subaltern of the corps and he now obtains promotion to the rank of Captain in t' e South Lancashire Regiment. Lieutenant Woods has been over eight years in the corps. He was appointed second lieutenant in January 1892, and promoted lieutenant in October 1894. Lieutenant M. A. Ord will also, it is expected, be promoted captain in the South Lancashire Regi- ment. He joined the army the same time as Lieut. Woods and was promoted lieutenant in November 1894.

Co!onel Macfie, C.B , commanding the 3rd Volun- teer Battalion Liverpool Regiment at Southport has retired from the service atter being at the head of the corps for the long period of 20 years having been appointed Lieutenant-Colonel of the old 13th Lancashire R.V. as far back as July 1880. fie was recognised as one of the most capable of the Lanca- shire Volunteer commanding officers and general regret is expressed that he has had to sever his con- nection with the cDrps of which he was so long the chief.

Nlajor J. Formby, another well-known Lancashire officer, has been promoted Lieutenant Colonel and appointed to command the corps at Southport in succession to Colonel Alacde, C.B. Lieutenant Colonel Formby, has been many years in the batta- lion and has been Major second in command for over 15 years, having been advanced to field rank in February 1885. Captain W. J. Rigg, will also, it is expected be promoted major in the 3rd Volunteer Battalion Liverpool Regiment, and will become second in command of the corps. He has been over ten years in command of a company becoming Cap- tain in February, 1890.

CURIOUS LICENSING OBJECTION

AT ST. HELENS. A Sprinter's Application.

At St. Helens, on Tuesday, John Banks, a well-known sprinter in the district,applied fora temporary authority to carry on the Nancy Inn, Pocket Nook-street, lately kept by John Whitfield. Inspector Steele, in the absence, of; Chief Constable Wood, obj.cted to the license oh curious grounds. Anticipating the objection, Mr E. W. Swift, solicitor, who appeared for applicant, said he understood the objection was that Banks had been a running man who had participated in races. He did not know if there was a suggestion that Banks was a betting man ; but he had been isstructed that he was not friendly to gambling men, and there was no reason to suppose he would attach them to the house if the license were granted. Inspector Steele said he objected to the temporary authority being granted on the groand that applicant's occupation and his associates rendered him an unsuitable person to hold a license. He had been a race-runner, and the nature of his employment late brought him into contact with betting men. He (Mr. Steele) had no objection to running so long as it was conducted apart from betting and gambling ; but it was almost invari- ably associated with those vices, and he contended that if the license were granted the house would be a place ef resort for betting men. a Applicant gave evidence in support of his case, and said he was twenty-eight years of age, and had served his thus at Pilkington's, afterwards going to act as bar- man for John Fletcher at the Railway Hotel. He stayed there three or four months, and then went to Smith's mineral water works: After that he wet back to the Railway Hotel for three or four years, and had since been at the Rifle Corps Ina. It was two years since he gave up running. Inspector Steele said if the magistrates wished to go into the case he would like to call evidence. The BEl:let' decide I that, as a special transfer sessions will be held in a fortnight, the matter should be ad- journed to then. Mr. Swift agreed to this course.

Good. Templars' .Convention.

The half-yearly meeting of the No. 1 Convention, South-West Lancashire, of the Independent Order of Good Templets was held on Saturday in the Co- operative Hall, Leigh. Representatives and visitors were present from lodges in Warrington, Earlestown. Ashton-in-Makerfield, Leigh, Tyldesley, Sutton and St. Helens. Bro Clarke, UC T, read the .quarterly report, which showed that good *ork was being done throughout the Convention area, open-sir meetings having been held at Warrington, St. Helens, tEarlestown and Haydock. The Secretary's report showed a slight decrease in membership. Bro E Newman, Earlestown read the Treasurer's report, showing a small balance in hand. Sister Mrs Woods, CSJ T (Earlestown), reported 1 that th3re had been a decrease of 21 members among the juveniles, but the prospects were brightening, as one new temple had been instituted at Warrington, and another was about to be formed at the same I place. The election of officers was then proceeded I with, and resulted as follows :—C CT, Bro G Clarke, Earlestown ; C Coun, Bro Kirwin, Leigh ; CV T, Sister Mrs Sheath, Warrington; CS J,T Sister Mrs Woods, Earlestown; C BS, Bro Brooks, Leigh; C Sec, Bro E Newman, Earlas- town ; C Treas. Gospel Hall.

On Sunday, the service was conducted by Mr. F. Broadhurst -who spoke on the offerings of Cain and Abel, (Gen. 4 145). This incident he said, was one of the most important in the lives of Cain and Abel. They had doubtless heard of their father's fall and separation from God's presence, also of the promise given of a Deliverer and how sacrifices must be offered to make them accept- able before God. The story told had given them the desire to make offerings to become accepted,the day had at last arrived. Cain's was a beautiful offering, every- thing perfect and pleasant, the result of hard toil and he felt proud of it and brought it forward exultingly, comparing very favourably with the simple childish offering of his despised brother, a wee spotless lamb. lie forgot one little thing though. The source of his offering was cursed on acclunt of sin. He brought to God a cursed thing, never acknowledging his own guilt and unworthiness to approach God. It was rejected. Man to-day brought his own self-righteouness and good works for God's acceptance but nothing short of God's Lamb,the Lord Jesus Christ,who died for us,cou4l make us acceptable and fit to spend EternityA the Paradise of God.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY, AUGUST 17, 1900.

EARLESTOWN & NEWTON.

EARLESTOWN PRIMITIVE METHODISTS. Special services in connection with the anniver- sary of the above C iapel was held on Sunday. The pulpit was occupied by the Rev. W. H. Davenport, and there were fair congregations. Special hymns and anthems were sung by the choir,and collections were made in aid of the Trust Funds. POLICE CASES. At the Newton-le-Willows Police Court, before Colonel Wood, on Monday morning, John Alker was charged with being drunk and disorderly at Earles- town on Saturday, and was fined 2s 61 and costs.— W. Woods was also charged with a like offence at Newton on Friday. and was fined 23 61 and costs. At the Newton Police Court on Tuesday before Nlr T. J. Gillespie, Charles Jones, hawker, and Benjamin Gentle, gipsy, were charged with being drunk in Newton on Monday, and were fined 2s 6d and costs each. MESSRS. MCCORQUODALE AND CO's. EXCURSIONS. The annual excursions promoted for the benefit of the employees of Messrs. McCorquodale and Co. at Newton took place on Saturday in beautiful weather. A couple of trains left for Blackpool at 5.55 and 6.ls,conveying about 1,150 passengers and about 500 left for Liverpool ail 630. The excursion- ists for Blackpool found that popular resort pretty well crowded, and much inconvenience was suffered in attending the various places of amusement, and the piers. Despite this they thoroughly enjoyed themselves, and after a most pleasant day they left Blackpool at 9.48 and 11.28,the second train reaching Earlestown and Newton shortly after one o'clock on Sunday morning. Those who took the shorter trip Ito Liverpool, passed their time in rambling about the city; whilst a very large number betook them- selves to New Brighton, Eastham Szc, where they enjoyed themselves equally as much as those who travelled to Blackpool. GOOD TEMPLARY. The Hope of Earlestown Lodge meeting was held in Wargrave Hall, on Monday.—Bro. Woods, L.D., presiding. Circular received " Good Templar 1 Orphanage," Sunbury-on-Thames; notice of District Lodge to be held at Liverpool ; invitation accepted to visit the Ashton Onward Lolge on September 12th.—Bro. Newman gave the report of No. 1 Con- vention held at Leigh.—The Secretary's report showed that while there had been a slight decrease owing to the light nights and the fine weather, the programme had been carried out. Three open meet- ings had been held and three members initiated.— Toe report of the S.J.T., showed that good work had been done, the programme having been ably i carried out, and thanks were due to those who had so ably assisted. An open-air meeting having been ' held on the market, a visitor briefly addressed the Lodge. It was resolved that the lodge be open to friends on the visit of the mission choir. The pro- gramme was" A night with Ireland," Bro Woods responsible. Song, " Killarney," Sister Woods; reading," Patsy among the Spirits," Bro Woods; reading by Bro Driug ; piano solo, Bro Willis.— Sister Newman read a letter from a friend in South Africa, humorously describing his experiences and observations in Pietermaritzburg. Owing to the lateness of the hour, several items were left over, a vote of thanks being accorded to Bro. Woods and the lodge was closed by Bro. Cutler, C.T. THE EARLESTOWN HombAYs. This week Earlestown wears a somewhat deserted appearance, owing to the holding of the annual holiday. Practically the holidays commenced on Friday evening last, with the closing of the Vulcan Foundry and Messrs. Slee and Co.'s Works, until next Monday. It was originally intended that the holidays should commence generally on Tuesday night, August 14th, for the remainder of the week, but the Vulcan men were not satisfied with four days and insisted upon the full weak, consequently they broke away from the arrangement. The Viaduct Works, and Messrs T and T Vicars adhered to itand their respective works closed on Tuesday night for the week. On Tuesday night until nearly midnight, Queen-street, Earlestown, presented an unusual appearance, the approaches to the station being on some occasions during the night crowded with people loaded with boxes and portmanteaus, all bent on hurrying away from the scene of their labours. Of course the majority were from the Viaduct Works, the men there by reason of the quarter fare privilege being enabled to betake tnemselves longer distances. Dublin, North Wales, London, Paris, and even Killarney were the ob- jectives of those who sought the train on Tuesday night. Many got away to Blackpool, but a big crowd waited until Wednesday morning before journeying to tht. Lancashire seaside resort. There were many left behind who could not make it con- venient to go away for any lengthened period, but even they took advantage of the holidays and Southport,Liverpool,Manchester,and New Brighton saw a large number of Earlestown people. By a stroke of good fortune fine weather has favoured the holidays, and consequently the enjoyment of them will be much enhanced. The holidays have been eagerly looked forward to, and it is confi- dently anticipated that next year, the full week will belgenerally given.

A CHILD DROWNED AT ST. HELENS.

The body of a cnild named Forshaw, aged seven, son of a collier, living at Newton-road, St. Helens, was re- covered from the St. Helens and Sankey Canal on Tuesday morning. On Monday, the child was sent home from Blackbrook School unwell,but never reached home. It was the child's mother who discovered the body in the canal, and it la presumed that while playing on the bank the boy fell in. THE FATAL FALL AT ST. HELENS.

On the 2nd instant Mrs Glynn° was in the yard of her house, when she slipped aril fell. Her ,njuries included a fractured arm. D. At the St. Helens County Courc on Monday his Honour Judge Goldthorpe beard a case in which Max Cohen, Limited, of Manchester, claimed £5 173 from Joseph Alen, a workingman, of Higher Parr-street. D3fendant hal obtained a watch from the plaintiffs at a cost of £6 15, to be repaid at 3s a fortnignt. ff had only re- paid 183, leaving the balance claimed. IMendant said he hid the watch valued, and it was only worth £2 15s at the outsid?. He was willing to forfeit the 18i he had paid if the plaintiffs would take the watch back. Plain- tiffs refused the offer. In reply to the judge,defendant said he had a wife and three children and earned 253 per week. His Honour coasiderel he had been very foolish in Vie matter, and ordered the payment of ths amount claimed at 2s 61 a month. FOOTPATH DISPUTE AT ST. HELENS.

At the meeting of the St. Helens Highways Committee on Wednesday afternoon, the Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. Rawlinson) reported correspondence that had passed between the Town Clerk and Mr Campbell, agent of Captain Michael Hughes, of Sherdley Park, with regard to a notice board that had been erected by order of the latter gentleman. The notice-board warned the public against taking vehicles, bicycles, or perambulators along a footpath leading from Mill-lane to Green-end. The Town C:erit pointed out to Mr. Campbell that the foot- path was a public one. and Mr. Campbell then replied that he had not disputed that the footpath was public. On the Town Clerk suggesting that the notice should be taken down, Mr. Campbell stated that as the board was erected by the special orders of Captain Hughes, he asked the Committee to wait until that gentiem In's return from South Africa. The Town Clerk agreed to this course.and the Committee confirmed his action. THE FATAL ACCIDENT AT A HAYDOCIC COLLIERY.

On Monday, Mr. S. Brighouse, culty coroner, held an inquest at the Waggon and Horses Hotel, Haydack; on the body of Robert Boardman. 44 years of age, of 8, Walker-street, St. Helens, who died in the Haydock Cottage Hospital, on Thursday last, from injurios re- ceived on the 2nd inst at the Princess Pit, owned by Messrs. Richard Evans and Co. Mr. H. Hall, the Government Inspector of Mines, was present, and Mr. Peace representel the firm, It was stated thal on the date named Boardman attempted to Enter a descending cage before it was brought to a standstill, and he was badly crushed about the head and shoulders. No , one witnessed the occurrence, to which attention was ! drawn by Boardman's screams. Evidence was given that no one had a right to enter a cage whilst it was in motion. John Hughes, the signalman at the shaft bot- tom.was temporarily away when the accident happened. The jury, in returning a verdict of" Accidental death," cautioned Hughes that he ought not to leave his post under any circumstances when men were waiting to ascend the shaft.

rse CLERK reported receipt of the following letter from the L3cal Government Board:—" I am directed by the Local Government Board to request that you will be vod enough to insert in ths enctosed statement par- ticulars as to the rateable value according to the valua- tion lists in force at Lady Day, 1887, of the portions of the Parishes iii Warrington, Latchford, and Poulton- with-Fearnbead which were added ta the Borough of Warrington in 1890 and 1896 respectively, and that you will retransmit tne statement at your earliest conveni- ence. AGED DESERVING POOR. INTERESTING CIRCULAR FROM THE LOCAL GOVERNHENT BOARD.

The CLERK said he had received front the Local Government Board a circular letter dealing mainly with the question of the removal of children feom the Work- lentlle and the provision of homes for the aged deserving poor. The letter, after stating that the question of the removal of imbeciles must be deferred for the pres- ent, proceeds as follows :—"The removal of children, whice was also rec3mmeaded by the Committee, hes been still more strongly urged upon the B card. It is a question on which public opinion is undoubtedly in accord with the me )mmendation of the Committee. and I am directed to urge upon Boards of Guardians to let no opportunity pass of carrying such an arrangement into effect. Tno opportunity will frequently arise when there is a question of enlarging the infirmary wards of the work- house, or of increasing the accommodation in some other respect, and when it may be felt that the wisest course would be to provide the extra accommodation needed by removing the cnildren altoeether fro n the workhouse,. In connection with this point the Board may observe that by the provision of cottage homes, by the hire of scattered homes, by boarding-out and emigration, ample means are afforded by which children may be entirely removed from association with the workhouie and work- house surroundings." With regard to the treatment of the aged deservine poor, the letter proceeds :—" It has been felt that per- -5 hes who have habitually led decent and deserving lives should, if they require relief in their old age, receive different treatment from those whose previous habits and characters have been unsatisfactory, and w, o h aye tailed to exercise thrift in the brineine up of their families or otherwise. The Board consider that aged deserving persons should not be urged to enter the Workhouse at all, unless there is some cause which renders such a course necessary, such as infirmity of mind or body; the absence of house accommodation, or of a suitable person to care for them, or some similar cause, but that they should be relieved by having adequate outdoor relief granted to them. The Board are happy to think that it is commonly the practice of Boards of Guardians to grant outdoor relief in such cases, but they are afraid chat too frequently such relief is not adequate in amount. They are desirous of pressing upon the Guardians that such relief should, when granted, be always adequate. When, however, it is necessary that such persons should receive indoor relief. the Beard consider that they might be granted certain privileges which could not be accorded to every inmate of the workhouse. The Board may avail themselves of this occasion to state that they have received a memorial I from members of the Council and the Exec:wive Committee of the National Association for Promoting the welfare of the feeble-minded, asking them to take steps to institute an inquiry into the number and con- dition of feeble-minded persons in receipt of poor law relief. By' feeble-minded 'persons the late memorial ists state that they' desire t 3 describe persons who are of weak intellect but are not certificable as imbeciles or insane, and who cannot, therefore, be detained in work- houses or elsewhere except at their own pleasure.' Tne ISoard would feel obliged if the Guardians would state the number of persons in the workhouse who could, in the opinion of the medical officer,be regarded as coming within toe abave description ; and would supply any further information with regard to the csnditiou of such persons which they are able to afford." The CLERK said the matters referred to had been largely dealt with by the Union. Many Unions had taken no steps at all in tee matter. In reply to a letter from toe Council the Registrar-General replied that he was unable to entertain the proposal to increase the number of registration sub-districts, unless it were proposed and I strongly supported by the Warrington Guardians, who, 11 if any sub-division of the Sankey sub-district were effected would have to pay the additional cost of regis. tration which would result from the proposed sub-divi- sion. Elrlestown, for £2OO as a site for Relief office, relief c3mmittee room, and relieving officer's residence, for the No. 2 or Newton district of the Union. He als) moved a resolution that the plans submitted by MaEsrs. Owen, arcoitects, for the erection of the relief office and, at an estimated cost of £1,700, having been approved by the Committag, they be recommended to the Board for adoption. He remarked that the price of material was somewhat up.and therefore the estimate of the architect was a little more than they had antici• pated. The cost had also b.eni a little increased by reason of the site being in a c =ntral position, and the baildings surrounding it being of an important charac- ter.

Dr. PATTERSON secondel. FORSHAW said he could not help thiqying that the present was the wrong time to build the Offices. The cost of the material was at least 20 per cent more than it was when prices were at the normal point, and the addirional cost of the building would be somathing like £4OO. The job would cost altogether about £2,000. Many firms were not proceeding with building operations for the reason he had mentioned. He thought £2,000 was to) big a sum to spend enecially as the Board tied borro %cid so mucn money, and he moved as an amend- ment that the matter be deferred for twelve months. Mr. HIBBERT seconded. Mr. ASHCROFT supported the amendment, remarking that the Board was already considerably in debt. Mr. PILLING : We have plenty of money. Mr. BICNT also considered that the matter should be deferred. He thought the estimate was quite £7OO too much. They ought to be able to ereat the building for 21.000. Mr. HICKMAN supported the resolutions of the Committee. He said it was ridiculous to suppose that a building suited to their requirements, to cover the area of land they desired to purchase, could be erected for £l,OOO, as Mr. Bent had suzgested. The Committee, after thrashing the whole matter out, had tinanirricu,ly come to the conclusion that the scheme now submitted should be adopted. He wanted to know how long they should have to wait before the price of material was less thae it was at present. The price of coal was going up, and coal would rule the price of building material very considerably. They might have to wait 20 years before the price of Iduilding material came down to its normal level.

Mr. W. HOUGHTON in supporting the propositions said he felt very strongly that they might have to wait a long time before the cost of building material was appreciably reduced. Bet.ides that, they would have other work ; they would be very glad to have the cost of building material reduced in time for the erection of the officea they had in project in Warrington. In regard to the Newton relief offices they at present paid a high rental for very indifferent accommodation, and he thought that there should be an alteration in that re- spect. Mr. PILLING. who also supported the resolutions, said that on land hk that referred to they could not get a building worth looking at without spending something Lke tlao amount the committee proposed to upend. The resolution was eventually passed, six members voting for the amendment.

Toe Me:licit Officer read his quarterly report for the quarter ended June 30th. There were registered births, 72 males, 62 females ; deaths, 27 males, 31 females ; excess of births, 45 males, 31 females. The births were at an annual rate of 34 7, and the deaths 15 05 per 1,000 of the estimated population. The infantile death- rate was 102 per cent and the deaths of children under 5 years of age 50 per cent, of the total number of deaths. The zymotic sick-rate—aotifted cases—was 1 6 per thousand. Fourteen of the above 25 cases were removed to the isolation hospital. There were seven deaths from the principal zymotic diseases, being less than one per 2,000 of the population. It; was resolved that the attention of the London and North-Western Railway Company be drawn to the con- dition of the approach to the goods station at Newton- le- Willows.

Evans and Co.,Limited,having reference to the adoption of Clarence-street after consideration of which it was resolved that it be a recommendation to the Council that Clarence-street be ad3pted as a highway repairable by the inhabitants at large. With reference to the question of huts on the Market, which hld b3eo referred back to this committee by the Council for further cocsideration, it was resolved that huts be allowed on the Market and placed in such positions as the Council may determine, and that they be fixed on sleepers,or other wood frame, as the Council may approve, in such a manner as will allow of a clear space of at least ten inches between the ground and the underside of the floor of the hut, and subject to an agreement being entered into between the occupiers and the Council ; and that the Surveyor submit at the next meetinc, of this committee a list of conditions which should be embodied in the agreement for consideration of the committee. Resolved that L: be a recommendation to the Council that those persons using any building, of whatever description, within the district, as a slaughter house, without the same being licensed and registered as a slaughter house by the Council, be served with 14 days' notice within which to make ap- plication to the Council for a license, and failing such license, legal proceedings to be taken against those per- sons continuing to use any such building as a slaughter house after the date of the expiration of the said notice.

The minutes wer pasied without comment. RESIONATION OF MR. SHIRLEY. COMPLIMENTARY REFERENCE 3.

a meeting of the GNieral Purposes Committee, a letter dated the 11'h July, was read from Mr. W. W. Shirley, resigning his position as Clerk to the Cquacil, as he was about to commence business on his own account. After cprisidsration thereof, it was resolved that this meeting accepts, with very sincere regret, Mr. Shirley's vsignation.—The Chairmen of Committees were .poointed to select six candidates to appear before the General Purposes Committee. At a further meeting of the committee held on the 13th August, it was resolved that Mr. C. Cole, of Handsworth, be recommended to the Council for appointment as clerk at a salary of £lBO per annum.

The Chairman, in moving the confirmation of the l minutes said he would take that opportunity of express- ing the Council's sincere regret on the resignation which had been received from Mr. Shirley. They never had a better officer and they would have great difficulty in renlacing him. They had appointed a man that day who promised well and with Mr. Shirley's assistance— for he had kindly offered to stop until the new man had got into his ways of work—he thought they would manage fairly well. He could only again express the Council's great respect for Mr. Shirley and wish him every prosperity in the new line in life he was about to begin. Personally he was deeply indebted to Mrt Shirley for the way he had carried out the work while he (11r. Collingwood) had been vice-chairman of tha Council. The Council owed Mr. Shirley a great deal fo the way he h3d kept them all straight on many occasion and they would have a difficulty in replacing him. Mr. Wood said he could endorse what the Chairman had said. As Chairman of the School Attendance Com- mittee he (Mr. Wood) could bear testimony to the great assistance the comrnitteF: had received from Mr. Shirley and to the value of his work in helping them in cases of great difficulty. Ho could only hope that Mr Shirley would 131 prosperoas in the new undertaking in which he was about to engage. He was sure they would all be able to look back with pleasure at the very pleasant time Mr. Shirley had spent amongst them. (Hear, hear.)

Mr. Smith speaking as the youngest member of the Council and as one who knew something of the duties that had to be done by the clerk of a council like their's observed that he had very great plea- sure in supporting the remarks which bad been made and to personally express 1118 regret at the Council losing

the services of a clerk such as Mr. Shirley had been. As far as he had been able t,4 observe since he was con- nected with the Council, Mr. Shirley possessed a thorough knowledge of his duties and always p9rformed them with the greatest zeal and ability. They all united in wishing him every success in tivy new walk of life he had chosen to adopt, and one could only regret that the very great knowledge he had obtained of Local Government law and practice would not be so useful to him in big new career as it would have been to the Council if they had had the pleasure of retaining his services. He endorsed what had been said in praise of Mr. Shirley's work.

Mr. Ormand said as chairman of the Finance Com- mittee he had had a great deal to do with Mr. Shirley in matters of accounts and the work of the Council generally, and he would like to state publicly with what satisfaction and pleasure it had been to work with him at all times in the domain of finance, and also to receive the assurance of the Local Government Board auditors of the satisfactory way in which the accounts had been kept. He was glad to say that during Mr. Shirley's tenure of office as clerk, there had not been a question of surcharge, and he thought that in itself was a very creditable thing to the Clerk himself and the Committee generally. He could only wish with the other members that Mr. Shirley might have every success and pros- perity in his new venture, and they hoped he would be spared to enjoy it. (Hear, hear.) The Council was losing an important servant, and it was due to Mr. Shirley that they should let him know that he went away from them with their sincere regret, and that they thoroughly appreciated all he had done for the Council in particular and the ratepayers generally. (Hear, hear). Mr. Shirley, in acknowledging the compliments, said he was very much obliged for the kind words which the Chairman and the several speakers had given ex- pression to with regard to his services as clerk to that authority. It was somewhat embarassing to sit there and listen to those very kind words, and he could say,in reply, that it was a real pleasure to him to feel that he had given satisfaction with his work in the past. He had always tried to do that and he was very glad to know that the Council appreciated what he had dene. He was obliged to the Council for wishing him success in his new venture. The prospects appeared to be good and he trusted that the good wishes which had been expressed would be fulfilled. He would now thank them heartily for the kind and encouraging words which had been given utterance to. GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE. PROPOSED _ELECTRIC SUPPLY.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Cricket. Warrington were again defeated on Saturday when they engaged Cheadle Hulme away from hcme. The visitors placed a weak eleven in the field,Messrs Clarke, Briggs, and Thompson all being absent. The home cap- I tain won the toss, and on the slow wicket put Warring. ton in, B Si!cock and J Callon being opposed by Teather and G E Radford. The start was disastrous,- for with only two runs on I the score sheet, 'Feather broke through Silceck's defence. Ca:lon quickly added nine, when the home professional met with another success, the retiring batsman having made all the runs scored. With Dr Smith and E Connor together, both batsmen forced the pace and a couple of fine drives by Connor were excellent strokes. At 26 Coanor was bowled, and Thewlis being sent back without scoring, the game took a turn in Chead le's favour. W El Podmore joined Dr. Smith, and both batsmen played the bowling confidently, the new comer pulling Radford for four and two from successive deliveries. At 36 Teather bowled Dr.- Smith, and at the same total the visiting captain returned one to the bowler. A useful stand was made by Duckworth, who assisted Podmore to take the total to 48, when the latter was out to a wonderful catch. His innings of 19 was a valu- able contribution, especially when the nature of the wicket is considered. Duckworth continued to score freely, until at 60 he was bawled off his pads. Hampson was out to his potent " leg glide" stroke, failing to time a straight one from Redfern, and the innings closed for 64. Cheadle Halm commenced in disastrous style for ,with only a run scored Harding was bowled. -- However a fine stand was made s hen A. B. Rome joined G. E. Radford. Thewlis was freely hit, and at 24 J. W. Bibby took the ball, and the change at once checked the rate of run getting. At 34 Rome was caught in the slips, whilst from the next ball E. A. Radford was also taken by Dr. Smith. Teather joined Radford, but saw the initial batsman have his middle peg dis- turbed by Dr. Smith. Teather scored freely, and Thewlis resumed, but was promptly despatched to the boundary. Bibby continued to bowl in splendid form, and runs from him only came at rare intervals. At 55 Williams was caught at mid-on but G. E. Haworth stayed with Teather until the latter made the winning hit. Teather was sent back by- Gibson at 69,f0r a vigorously hit 26. The remaining batsmen did next to nothing and ' the last thirteen overs, sent down yielded only a single. ' The innings closed for 74. J. W. Bibby came out with some remarkable figures, his seven wickets costing but 15 runs. --- Angling. The ninth annual COLF.eSt for the Worsloy Canal Fishery championship was decided in favourable weather at Worsley on Saturday. There were 341 competitors, the majority of whom were from Manchester and War- rington. The fishing extended from four o'clock until six under the supervision of Mr. J. Mayhall (Warring— ton), who officiated as referee. The result was declared as follows :—First prize, value £4. carrying a gold medal and the championship for 1900 won by J. Brown (Sale), weight 143 z. ; 2ad, value £3 cash and gold- centre medal, J. Davis (Leigh), 133 z. 4Jr. ; 3rd and 4th a tie, aggregate value £3 in cash, to lam divided between Jesse Sumner (Leigh), and J. Allen, jun. (Pendleton), each being 121 z. 812--dr. ; the sth. value 17s 63, J. Sykes (Swinton), 9)z. 9ar.

Alderman FRANCOMB : If we have got.to pay the sewer rate we think you ought to find some means of taking the sewage. The CLERK: But you have not got to pay it. It iS finished with. Alderman FRANCOME : Would it be a difficult thing for you to join the Corporation sewers at Longford ? The CLERK: I don't know. Alderman FE&NCOMB said it was a state of things that ought to be altered. Property built on land of his own at Winwick was in a frightful state. The whole of the land was water-logged in wet weather. The question was whether it would be a difficult manner to drain into the Warrington Corporatian sewers. Mr. THORNTON doubted whether the levels would be right. Some further discussion ensued the Clerk stating if they were a large urban district it would be a small matter to undertake the expense. Mr. MITSPRATT : We quite appreciate your situation.' The CLERK said the rateable value of Winwick would be of very small value to the parish. It would be of considerable value to that Council and to the Union and also to the County, but the parish itself would not benefit because the Council raised their calls in proportion to the rateable value. After consultation with the members of the deputa- tion, Alderman FRANCOMB said they were of opinion that it would be much better to adjourn the matter for a time, and perhaps the Council would grant them another in- terview later on. In the meantime the Council could make all the inquiries they could, and the Board would do the same. It might also be that they would be able to make a grant if the Council had to take the sewage. They were quite willing to bear any expenses the Coun- cil were put to. This was all the business.

Local Receiving Order. A receiving order has been made under the Bank- ruptcy Acts in the case of John Nicol, watchmaker, Buttermarket-street, Warrington. 'Ow At Altrincham, on Wednesday, Isaac Barnett, com- mercial traveller, Altrincham, was charged with hawk- ing cigars in the street at Sale on Tuesday afternoon. Defendant offered to sell boxes of cigars to a constable in plain clothes, and also went to several shops and tried to dispose of them. Defendant said he represented a cigar firm, and had been getting orders from retailers. He was ignorant of the law, and this was his first offence. Defendant was find £5 NEW ST. HELENS MAGISTRATES. The Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster (Lord J3M0.1 of Hereford), has added Mr. Joseph Massey and Mr. Richard Austin Pilk,ngton to the Commission of tbe Peace for St. Helens.—Mr. Joseph Massey is a local solicitor, one of the most recently elected aldermen of the Borough Council, and a very prominent and active Conservative. He has a record of considerable service in the Council, particularly as deputy-chairman of the Health Committee. The Inew magistrate, Mr. Richard Austin Pilkington, is the eldest son of Colonel W W Pilkington, of the Haz4s. Prescot. a senior member of the great glass firm of Pilkington Brothers. Mr R A Pilkington is quite a young man. He is regarded as a Unionist, though he has taken little part in local politics. He has been some two years or so on the Library Com- mittee. He will be the youngest magistrate on the St. Helens bench. !MST BOOTS FO ALL,NUNDER THE CLOCK.— rt HOW TO INCREASE YOUR INCOME (problem solved) twenty additional agents required. excellent terms to ener getic men of good character (whole or spare time).—Apply W Taylor,Superintendent Wesleyan General Assurance Society 14, Cairo-street. To secure more visitors, several towns, remarkable for their enterprise, are claiming the proud title of " Queen of Water- ing Places."
